computer-science.bigb
= Computer science
{wiki}

Computer science studies how information can be represented and transformed by <algorithms>, and what those transformations can achieve. Its central questions connect <mathematics> to practical design: what answer should a procedure produce, why is it correct, and how much work does it require as its <input> grows? <Computability theory> asks which tasks can be solved at all; <computational complexity theory> asks what resources a solution needs.

The beauty is that choosing a representation can change what is feasible. Finding a name among a million entries sounds like a million checks. Store the entries in a <sorted array>, and <binary search> needs at most twenty middle-entry comparisons. The order makes each unsuccessful comparison eliminate a whole region of possibilities. A <data structure> supplies useful organization; an <algorithm> exploits it; <time complexity> explains the resulting savings.

<Algorithm correctness> covers every permitted <input>, including absent names and empty collections. <Time complexity> predicts scaling; <statistical program profiling> investigates a particular implementation.

\Include[computer-science-author]
\Include[computer-science-bibliography]

= Computer graphics
{parent=Computer science}
{wiki}

= Raster graphics
{parent=Computer graphics}
{wiki}

= Raster image
{synonym}

<Raster graphics> describe a picture by an <array> of <pixels> on a grid. For a rectangular image of width $W$ and height $H$, write its stored values as $p_{rc}$, with $0\leq r<H$ and $0\leq c<W$. A <grayscale> <pixel> can have one shade value; a colour <pixel> can have several component values. The grid specifies where values belong, while the encoding specifies what those values mean. A <raster image> therefore needs dimensions and an interpretation of its samples, not just a string of numbers.

The appealing feature is that pictures become explicit data: selecting a rectangle selects entries of an <array>; comparing two images compares their <pixel> values; <image compression> can exploit repeated or related values. <Raster sampling and reconstruction> explains why the stored grid and the displayed picture are different objects. A square drawn around a sample is one display convention, not a claim that the original scene consisted of little squares.

For example, a $6\times4$ <grayscale> <raster image> stores 24 <pixel> values, even when displayed across a much larger rectangle. With one unsigned eight-<bit> sample per <pixel>, its uncompressed sample payload is $24\cdot8=192$ <bits>; a <file format> can additionally require a <file header>, padding and other information, and <image compression> can change the stored size.

Enlarging that image can copy each <pixel> into a block through <nearest-neighbor interpolation>, or infer intermediate values through <interpolation>. Either procedure supplies a reconstruction rule; neither determines which unrecorded detail was present in the original. <Vector graphics> use a different representation, specifying geometric objects to be rendered onto a chosen grid. Re-rendering such a description and enlarging an existing <raster image> are different operations.

= Pixel
{parent=Raster graphics}
{wiki}

A <pixel> is one picture element at an indexed position in a <raster image>. It can contain a single <grayscale> value or several colour components. The word can also refer to a physical display element; the indexed element of the stored image is the meaning used here. One stored <pixel> need not occupy exactly one physical display element when the image is resized. Microsoft's bitmap documentation likewise distinguishes an image's grid and its numerical colour representation.https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/gdi/about-bitmaps[Microsoft Learn, About Bitmaps: pixel grids and bitmap representations]{ref}

Separate \i[position] from \i[value]: <raster sampling and reconstruction> concerns the spatial grid, while <sample depth> concerns how many <bits> encode each component. Four times as many <pixels> can describe a finer grid without improving the <quantization (signal processing)> of any value. Conversely, more <bits> per sample can distinguish more shades without locating an edge more precisely.

In the binary example under <run-length encoding>, one <bit> per <pixel> selects black or white. In the <grayscale> example under <lossy block averaging>, eight <bits> per <pixel> select one of 256 codes. Calling them codes keeps their numerical values distinct from the physical light that a display emits.

= Sample depth
{parent=Pixel}
{title2=$b,\quad 2^b$}

The <sample depth> $b$ is the number of <bits> allocated to one stored sample. An unsigned $b$-<bit> <integer> has $2^b$ codes, $0,\ldots,2^b-1$. This is per component, not automatically per <pixel>: three eight-<bit> components require 24 payload <bits> per <pixel>. A <raster image> with $W H$ <pixels> and $k$ such components requires $WHkb$ payload <bits> before <data compression>, padding or a <file header>.

For a toy normalized shade $x\in[0,1]$, define uniform <quantization (signal processing)> with endpoints included by
$$
q_b(x)=\left\lfloor(2^b-1)x+\frac12\right\rfloor,\qquad \widehat x_b=\frac{q_b(x)}{2^b-1}.
$$
The <floor function> implements rounding to the nearest code, with exact halves rounded upward. The reconstruction step is $\delta_b=1/(2^b-1)$, and $|\widehat x_b-x|\leq\delta_b/2$. For $b=2$, the levels are $0,1/3,2/3,1$; for $b=8$ they are $0,1/255,\ldots,1$. Increasing <sample depth> refines this value grid without changing the spatial grid. These levels are uniform in $x$; if $x$ is encoded <sRGB>, they are not uniform in <relative luminance>.

<PNG> uses a bit-depth field per sample or palette index. Its <grayscale> type without alpha permits 1, 2, 4, 8 or 16 <bits> per sample; its truecolour type permits 8 or 16 <bits> per component. These are format-specific restrictions, not a rule for every <raster image>.https://www.w3.org/TR/png-3/#11IHDR[W3C, PNG Specification, Third Edition, section 11.2.1, IHDR, Table 12]{ref}

= Grayscale
{parent=Raster graphics}
{wiki}

= Greyscale
{synonym}

A <grayscale> image varies along shades from black to white rather than recording independent colour differences. A numerical <grayscale> image assigns one shade sample to each <pixel>; an encoding determines how that sample maps to a displayed shade. A colour <raster image> can also look <grayscale>, for example when its three <sRGB> components are equal.

\Image[raster-pixels-grayscale-quantization.png]
{title=Toy quantization and sRGB encoding: more codes and more light are different questions}

The convention in our <lossy block averaging> example is black at the <integer> code 0 and white at 255. Its <arithmetic means> act on those stored codes. This is a fully specified numerical experiment, but it does not claim that code 128 means half the physical light of code 255. <PNG> explicitly allows samples that are not proportional to light: its <grayscale> interpretation depends on the indicated transfer curve, and can be device-dependent when that information is absent.https://www.w3.org/TR/png-3/#6Colour-values[W3C, PNG Specification, Third Edition, section 6.1: colour types, grayscale interpretation and transfer curves]{ref}

Two different questions therefore need different operations. To average \i[stored codes], average the <integers>. To average <relative luminance> for neutral <sRGB> shades, decode the codes to linear light, average there, and encode the result again. Equal weights on black and white give encoded value $1/2$ by the first method, but approximately $0.73536$ by the second. Rounded to eight-<bit> codes these are 128 and 188. This example concerns ideal neutral shades and the specified <sRGB> conversion; it does not assert a universal grayscale conversion for every colour encoding.

The upper panels hold the spatial grid fixed and change <sample depth>: four levels produce broad constant bands, while 256 levels make smaller numerical steps. The lower panels hold the colour interpretation fixed and compare encoded <sRGB> values with <relative luminance>. All panels are calculated synthetic examples, not measured display data. <Grayscale> is useful when shade structure is the quantity of interest: a synthetic scalar <image signal> can be inspected without introducing separate colour components.

= Raster sampling and reconstruction
{parent=Raster graphics}
{title2=$p_{rc}=f(x_c,y_r)$}

A point-sampling model assigns $p_{rc}=f(x_c,y_r)$ to a <pixel> on a grid, where $f$ is a <continuous function> representing an <image signal>. An area-sampling model instead assigns the <average value of a function> over a specified footprint. These are different models: a narrow feature between point samples can be missed, whereas its contribution to an area sample depends on its width and shade. Neither model says that the recorded value uniquely determines everything inside a grid cell.

\Image[raster-pixels-grayscale-sampling.png]
{title=A toy raster grid, two reconstructions, and distinct signals with identical samples}

The upper panels start with exactly the same $6\times4$ <array>. <Nearest-neighbor interpolation> repeats its values into a $24\times16$ display; successive <linear interpolations> in the two coordinate directions produce a smoother $24\times16$ display. Original sample positions are marked. Both displays use 384 output <pixels>, but only the same 24 recorded values. The extra values follow the chosen <interpolation> rule. Between-grid appearance is inferred, not independently recorded.

Spatial resolution needs a stated meaning. A grid over a rectangle of width $L_x$ and height $L_y$ has nominal cell pitches $\Delta x=L_x/W$ and $\Delta y=L_y/H$. Over the same rectangle, doubling both dimensions halves these pitches and quadruples the <pixel> count. That is a finer grid, not a guarantee of more recoverable detail. Blurring before sampling, <quantization (signal processing)> and <aliasing> can make different originals share the same recorded <array>. Enlarging the rectangle without adding samples instead increases the displayed pitch.

A reproducible computational demonstration makes the ambiguity exact. Take the one-dimensional profiles $f(x)=0.5+0.4\sin(2\pi x)$ and $g(x)=0.5+0.4\sin(10\pi x)$, repeated identically along every row. Sample at $x_j=j/4$ for $j=0,\ldots,8$. Since $10\pi x_j=2\pi x_j+2\pi j$, the fact that the <sine> is a <periodic function> gives $f(x_j)=g(x_j)$. Both yield $0.5,0.9,0.5,0.1,0.5,0.9,0.5,0.1,0.5$, although at $x=1/8$ they give approximately $0.78284$ and $0.21716$. The lower panel draws both originals and their common samples: no operation on those samples alone can identify the original profile. This is <aliasing> in an ideal point-sampling model, without <image noise> or rounding.

The <Nyquist–Shannon sampling theorem> describes reconstruction under a bandwidth restriction and ideal sampling assumptions; it does not grant uniqueness for arbitrary <image signals> from a finite <raster image>.https://fab.cba.mit.edu/classes/S62.12/docs/Shannon_noise.pdf[Shannon, Communication in the Presence of Noise (1949), section II, Theorem 1 and reconstruction from the infinite sample sequence]{ref} The beautiful lesson is constructive: choose what detail the grid should preserve before sampling, and choose a reconstruction rule suited to the information actually retained.

= sRGB
{c}
{parent=Computer graphics}
{wiki}

<sRGB> specifies a red-green-blue colour encoding. Restricting attention to component values in $[0,1]$, its encoded component $s$ corresponds to the linear-light component
$$
D(s)=\begin{cases}s/12.92,&0\leq s\leq0.04045,\\((s+0.055)/1.055)^{2.4},&0.04045<s\leq1.\end{cases}
$$
Thus the conversion is piecewise, not a single power law. The reverse conversion used here is
$$
E(l)=\begin{cases}12.92l,&0\leq l\leq0.0031308,\\1.055l^{1/2.4}-0.055,&0.0031308<l\leq1.\end{cases}
$$
These conventional rounded thresholds are used by the W3C conversion routines; they give a tiny mismatch at the join rather than a mathematically exact pair of inverses everywhere.https://www.w3.org/TR/css-color-4/#predefined-sRGB[W3C, CSS Color Module Level 4, section 10.2: encoded and linear-light sRGB]{ref}https://www.w3.org/TR/css-color-4/#color-conversion-code[W3C, CSS Color Module Level 4, sample colour-conversion code: lin_sRGB and gam_sRGB]{ref}

For an eight-<bit> neutral <grayscale> code $q$, use $s=q/255$ in all three components. Its <relative luminance> is then $D(s)$. For example, $D(0.5)\approx0.21404$, whereas $E(0.5)\approx0.73536$. This is why averaging encoded values and averaging linear light give different answers. <sRGB> is a specified interpretation; merely calling a <raster image> <grayscale> does not imply it uses <sRGB>.

= Relative luminance
{parent=sRGB}
{title2=$Y$}

For the normalized <sRGB> calculations here, <relative luminance> is the linear-light weighted sum
$$
Y=0.2126R+0.7152G+0.0722B,
$$
where $R,G,B$ are decoded linear-light components, not encoded code values. This is the <sRGB> calculation specified in W3C's WCAG 2.2 definition of <relative luminance>. It places reference black at 0 and reference white at 1; it does not by itself specify an absolute display light level.https://www.w3.org/TR/WCAG22/#dfn-relative-luminance[W3C, WCAG 2.2, definition of relative luminance: sRGB decoding and weights]{ref}

For a neutral <sRGB> shade, $R=G=B=l$, and the weights sum to 1, so $Y=l$. For a coloured <pixel>, <relative luminance> can be encoded as a neutral <sRGB> shade with all three encoded components $E(Y)$. This preserves the specified $Y$ while discarding colour differences; it is one precisely defined <grayscale> conversion, not a unique way to turn all colour images into <grayscale>.

= Vector graphics
{parent=Computer graphics}
{wiki}

<Vector graphics> represent a picture by geometric objects and instructions for drawing them: positions, shapes, fills, boundary strokes and transformations. A <circle> can be stored as a centre and radius; a <rectangle> as a corner, width and height; a <polygon> as an ordered list of vertices. These geometric primitives describe whole objects rather than assigning a colour to every <pixel>. <SVG> provides elements for such basic shapes and for paths assembled from <line segments> and curves.https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/shapes.html#Introduction[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 10.1: basic shapes and their equivalent paths]{ref}

The useful separation is between the stored geometry and the finite output. A <circle> of radius 2 remains the same geometric description whether rendered across twenty or two thousand output <pixels>. This is the sense in which <vector graphics> are resolution independent: increasing the output grid can evaluate the original geometry again. The resulting <raster image> still has finite spatial resolution, and numerical coordinates have finite precision. Enlarging a previously rendered <raster image> through <interpolation> instead works with its existing samples. Neither operation guarantees that arbitrarily thin details remain visible at every size.

An <SVG> path uses a current point and drawing commands. For example, `M 1 4 L 5 4 L 3 1 Z` moves to $(1,4)$, draws two <line segments>, then closes back to the starting point, giving a <triangle>. Uppercase commands use absolute <coordinates>; the corresponding lowercase commands use offsets from the current point. Thus `M 1 4 l 4 0 l -2 -3 z` describes the same <triangle>. The coordinates belong to the current user <Cartesian coordinate system>, not necessarily to output <pixels>.https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/paths.html#PathData[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 9.3: path data, current points and absolute or relative commands]{ref}

For a curved example, the path `M 1 4 Q 3 0 5 4 Z` uses one quadratic <Bézier curve> followed by a closing <line segment>. With <control points> $P_0=(1,4)$, $P_1=(3,0)$ and $P_2=(5,4)$, its curved boundary is
$$
B(t)=(1-t)^2P_0+2t(1-t)P_1+t^2P_2=(1+4t,\;4-8t+8t^2),\qquad 0\leq t\leq1.
$$
The endpoints are $P_0,P_2$, and $B(1/2)=(3,2)$; the interior <control point> $P_1$ is not a point on the curve. A fill paints the enclosed region, while a stroke paints a band around its boundary with a chosen width. One can use either or both: geometry and paint instructions are distinct.https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/paths.html#PathDataQuadraticBezierCommands[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 9.3.7: quadratic Bézier path commands]{ref}https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/painting.html#Introduction[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 13.1: filling interiors and stroking outlines]{ref}

\Image[vector-graphics-path-transform.png]
{title=Analytical path illustration: a quadratic Bézier boundary and its affine image}

The first illustration uses increasing $y$ downward, following the initial <SVG> coordinate convention. Its dashed <control polygon> guides the eye; it is not part of the filled path. Apply the <affine map>
$$
T(x,y)=(1.5x+0.5y+1,\;y+1),\qquad A=\begin{pmatrix}1.5&0.5\\0&1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
This combines horizontal scaling, a <shear mapping> and a <translation>. The transformed <control points> are $(4.5,5)$, $(5.5,1)$ and $(10.5,5)$, giving the path `M 4.5 5 Q 5.5 1 10.5 5 Z`. Its curved midpoint is $T(3,2)=(6.5,3)$. The <affine equivariance of a geometric basis> explains why transforming only the <control points> transforms the entire <Bézier curve>: its weights sum to one, so the <translation> is added exactly once. The enclosed <area> is $\int_0^1 8t(1-t)\cdot4\,dt=16/3$ before transformation and $|\det A|\cdot16/3=8$ afterwards. These checks concern the filled region, excluding the width of any stroke.

An <SVG> `viewBox` specifies a <rectangle> in user <coordinates> to map into a display region. For example, `viewBox="0 0 6 6"` in a square viewport of 120 by 120 display units gives a scale of 20 display units per user unit; in a square viewport of 600 by 600 it gives 100. The path's <control points> need not change. For unequal proportions, `preserveAspectRatio` controls whether the mapping preserves proportions or stretches the axes separately. These display units need not correspond one for one to physical display <pixels>.https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/coords.html#InitialCoordinateSystem[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 8.4: initial axes and user units]{ref}https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/coords.html#ViewBoxAttribute[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, sections 8.6–8.7: viewBox mapping and preserveAspectRatio]{ref}

\i[Rasterization] converts the geometric drawing into a <raster image> on a chosen grid. Deciding whether each <pixel> centre lies inside a filled region is a simple rule, but a moving diagonal boundary can make entire <pixels> switch abruptly. \i[Antialiasing] reduces these sampling artifacts by accounting for a footprint around each sample, rather than treating every <pixel> as a single inside-or-outside decision. This connects <vector graphics> to <raster sampling and reconstruction> and <aliasing>; a finer grid and a better sampling rule solve different parts of the problem.

For an exact analytical model, fill the region $R=\{(x,y)\in[0,4]^2:y\leq x+1/4\}$. For a square <pixel> footprint $S$, let
$$
\alpha_S=\frac{\operatorname{area}(R\cap S)}{\operatorname{area}(S)},\qquad L_S=\alpha_S L_{\rm fill}+(1-\alpha_S)L_{\rm background}.
$$
Here $\alpha_S$ is geometric coverage, and $L$ is <relative luminance> for ideal opaque neutral shades, with $L_{\rm fill}=0$ and $L_{\rm background}=1$. The <intersection> and <area> determine how much of the footprint is covered. This is a specified area-average model, not a claim that every <SVG> renderer uses exact coverage. The <SVG> `shape-rendering` property supplies rendering hints and permits implementation tradeoffs, including disabling antialiasing for some choices.https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/painting.html#ShapeRenderingProperty[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 13.10.1: shape-rendering hints and antialiasing tradeoffs]{ref}

\Image[vector-graphics-raster-coverage.png]
{title=Analytical rasterization model: pixel-centre decisions versus exact coverage on two grids}

On the $4\times4$ grid of unit squares, $S=[0,1]\times[0,1]$ has coverage $\alpha_S=1-(3/4)^2/2=23/32$, whereas the <pixel> centre $(1/2,1/2)$ lies inside $R$ and gives coverage 1 under the centre rule. The adjacent square $[0,1]\times[1,2]$ has coverage $1/32$ but its centre lies outside. The entire filled <area> is $16-(15/4)^2/2=287/32$. Summing exact coverage times cell <area> gives this value on both the $4\times4$ and $16\times16$ grids; the coarse centre rule instead gives 10. Coverage values are displayed after <sRGB> encoding of the modeled <relative luminance>. The thin coloured line is a geometry overlay, not an extra sampled stroke. Enlarging the middle panel by <nearest-neighbor interpolation> would repeat its sixteen values; re-rendering the geometry produces the 256 independently evaluated footprints in the right panel. Antialiasing reduces abrupt edges but cannot make a finite grid preserve every geometric detail.

The attractive feature of <vector graphics> is editability at the level of intent: move one <control point> to bend a boundary, change a <circle>'s radius, or transform a whole diagram while keeping its parts aligned. Logos, technical diagrams and <outline fonts> benefit because their structure can be expressed with relatively few shapes and curves. A complicated photograph may require enormous geometric detail, so <vector graphics> do not imply a smaller file than <raster graphics>. Nor does an <SVG> container guarantee that every object is vector geometry: its `image` element can embed or reference a <PNG> <raster image>. Transforming that embedded <raster image> changes its placement and reconstruction, but does not recover missing original detail.https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/embedded.html#ImageElement[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 12.3: image elements and embedded raster content]{ref}

= SVG
{c}
{parent=Vector graphics}
{wiki}

= Scalable Vector Graphics
{c}
{synonym}

<SVG>, short for <Scalable Vector Graphics>, is a markup <file format> for two-dimensional graphics, specified by the W3C SVG Working Group. It describes <vector graphics> shapes, text and image objects, with grouping and transformations; it can therefore contain both geometric objects and <raster images>. The small command strings in <vector graphics> are path data within this format, not complete standalone documents.https://www.w3.org/TR/SVG2/intro.html#AboutSVG[W3C SVG Working Group, SVG 2, section 1.1: scope, graphical objects and transformations]{ref}

= File format
{parent=Computer science}
{wiki}

A <file format> specifies how a <binary string> is interpreted: field order, field widths, permitted values and where decoding stops. A <data compression> comparison must include all fields required to recover the data.

= File header
{parent=File format}

A <file header> is leading information describing how to interpret the following data. Dimensions and a choice of compression method are examples. The two toy <image compression> examples explicitly count these fields.

= Data compression
{parent=Computer science}
{wiki}

<Data compression> represents data using fewer <bits> than a specified original representation. The saving can reduce storage in a <file system> and reduce the number of <bits> sent through a <communication channel>. At a fixed transmission rate, fewer <bits> arrive sooner; this makes better use of a fixed transmission budget. Both the encoded data and the instructions needed to interpret them contribute to the size of a stored file.

There are two different promises. <Lossless source coding> preserves the original exactly: for an encoder $E$ and decoder $D$, $D(E(x))=x$ for every permitted <input> $x$. Thus $E$ must be an <injective function>. <Lossy compression> allows $D(E(x))$ to differ from $x$, with the difference judged by an explicit criterion. A program or numerical record may require exact recovery; a preview image may tolerate altered <pixels> in return for fewer <bits>.

The appeal of <lossless source coding> is that repetition can be described rather than copied. <Run-length encoding> says "repeat this value twelve times"; a <prefix code> can give more frequent symbols shorter descriptions. These are distinct ways to exploit structure, and neither promises savings for every <input>. <Image compression> supplies especially visible examples of both <lossless source coding> and <lossy compression>.

Claude E. Shannon's 1948 paper made the role of <probability distributions> precise. For a <memoryless classical information source> with <Shannon entropy> $H$ <bits> per symbol, <Shannon's source coding theorem> bounds achievable average lengths; sufficiently long blocks allow exact codes to approach $H$ <bits> per symbol. This is a statement about a specified source and average lengths, not a promise to shorten every message.<A Mathematical Theory of Communication by Claude Shannon>[Theorem 9 and its coding constructions]{ref}

= Universal lossless shortening is impossible
{parent=Data compression}

Fix $n\geq1$. There is no <injective function> from all length-$n$ <binary strings> to <binary strings> of length strictly less than $n$. There are $2^n$ possible <inputs>, but even allowing the empty string gives only
$$
\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}2^k=2^n-1
$$
possible shorter <outputs>. The <finite geometric series> and <pigeonhole principle> leave at least two <inputs> sharing an <output>, which prevents exact decoding. For $n=3$, eight <inputs> compete for the empty string and the six strings `0`, `1`, `00`, `01`, `10`, `11`.

This counting argument includes any <file header> or other information sent to the decoder. An <algorithm> chosen separately for each <input> must also be identified to the receiver; its description is not free. A useful compressor saves space on some <inputs> while leaving others unchanged or making them longer. <Lossy compression> avoids the exact-recovery requirement by deliberately allowing several <inputs> to share an <output>.

= Lossy compression
{parent=Data compression}
{wiki}

<Lossy compression> reduces a representation while permitting irreversible changes to the original. The encoder can map distinct <inputs> to the same <output>, so its decoder cannot recover both originals exactly. The useful question becomes how much storage is saved for an acceptable amount of distortion: <mean squared error> is one numerical criterion, but it does not by itself measure how noticeable a visual change is.

For example, storing the <arithmetic mean> of four <grayscale> <pixels> retains their average shade while discarding their arrangement. Blocks $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&100\\100&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ and $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}50&50\\50&50\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ both produce 50. The former has detail and the latter is uniform, yet the decoder receives the same value. <Lossy block averaging> makes this ambiguity, its <bit> saving and its <mean squared error> exact.

= Lossy block averaging
{parent=Lossy compression}
{title2=$q_{ij}=\lfloor m_{ij}+1/2\rfloor$}

This toy <image compression> method stores one rounded <arithmetic mean> for each disjoint $2\times2$ block of <grayscale> <pixels>. The <quantization (signal processing)> rule is round to the nearest <integer>, with exact halves rounded upward. Each stored value is an unsigned eight-<bit> <integer>. Reconstruction fills the original block with that value, a form of <nearest-neighbor interpolation>. Averaging already discards information; rounding can discard more.

Here is an exact $16\times16$ source <array>. Index rows and columns by $r,c\in\{0,\ldots,15\}$ and define
$$
\begin{aligned}
b_{rc}&=40+10\lfloor r/2\rfloor+12\lfloor c/2\rfloor,\\
x_{rc}&=b_{rc}+20(-1)^{r+c}+\mathbf{1}_{\{r\text{ even},\ c\text{ even}\}}.
\end{aligned}
$$
The <floor function> makes $b$ constant within each block; the <indicator function> adds 1 only to its upper-left <pixel>. All values are <integers> from 20 to 215. Each block therefore has the exact form
$$
\begin{pmatrix}b+21&b-20\\b-20&b+20\end{pmatrix},\qquad m=b+\tfrac14,\qquad q=\lfloor m+\tfrac12\rfloor=b.
$$
For example, the upper-left block $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}61&20\\20&60\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ has <arithmetic mean> $161/4=40.25$ and is stored as 40. The block immediately to its right $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}73&32\\32&72\end{smallmatrix}\right)$ is stored as 52. The complete stored $8\times8$ <array> is specified by $q_{ij}=40+10i+12j$ for $0\leq i,j\leq7$. Its first row is $40,52,64,76,88,100,112,124$; its last is $110,122,134,146,158,170,182,194$.

This toy <file format> again has eight-<bit> width and height fields followed by a one-<bit> mode: 0 means raw eight-<bit> <grayscale> <pixels>, 1 means the rounded block <arithmetic means>. This profile accepts only width and height 16. The decoder knows the block size and reconstruction rule from the format, reads the 64 stored values in row order, and repeats each across its corresponding $2\times2$ block. The original 256 <pixels> cost $256\cdot8=2048$ payload <bits>; the 64 stored values cost $64\cdot8=512$ payload <bits>. The reduction is $75\%$. These payload counts exclude the common 17-<bit> <file header>; complete sizes are 2065 and 529 <bits>, or 2072 and 536 <bits> after final zero padding to eight-<bit> units. There is no stored codebook or additional prediction rule.

The empirical <mean squared error> averages squared differences over the 256 original <pixel> positions:
$$
\operatorname{MSE}=\frac1{256}\sum_{r=0}^{15}\sum_{c=0}^{15}(x_{rc}-\widehat x_{rc})^2
=\frac{21^2+20^2+20^2+20^2}{4}=410.25.
$$
Every block has the same four errors, so this value is exact in squared stored shade units. If the unrounded <arithmetic mean> $b+1/4$ could be reconstructed, the error would instead be $410.1875$; eight-<bit> <quantization (signal processing)> adds $0.0625$. That hypothetical comparison does not count fractional means as free eight-<bit> values.

The broad shade pattern survives, while the alternating bright and dark <pixels> vanish. Indeed, a second source with every block constant at $b$ produces the identical stored <array> $q$: exact decoding of both sources is impossible. This makes the storage benefit and the lost detail visible at once. Block averaging is useful for a coarse preview when the individual <pixels> are dispensable; choosing an acceptable <mean squared error> or visual result is part of the application, not a guarantee supplied by the encoder.

\Image[lossy-block-image-comparison.png]
{title=Rounded 2 by 2 block means reduce the payload by 75 percent while destroying fine detail}

= Run-length encoding
{parent=Data compression}
{wiki}

= RLE
{c}
{synonym}

<Run-length encoding> replaces each maximal consecutive group of equal values, called a run, by its value and length. Decoding repeats each value the specified number of times. It is <lossless source coding>: no values are discarded. For a <binary string>, successive maximal runs alternate between 0 and 1, so storing the first <bit> and all run lengths is enough.

Take this $8\times8$ <raster image>, with 0 black and 1 white, read left to right across each row and then top to bottom:
```
00000000
00001111
11110000
00001111
11110000
00001111
11110000
00000000
```
Reading the whole image as one 64-<bit> <binary string> gives exactly seven runs:
$$
(\text{value},\text{length})=(0,12),(1,8),(0,8),(1,8),(0,8),(1,8),(0,12).
$$
The lengths sum to $12+8+8+8+8+8+12=64$. Repeating the alternating values for these lengths and splitting after every eighth <pixel> reconstructs every displayed row exactly.

Here is a complete toy <file format>. Its <file header> is an eight-<bit> unsigned width, an eight-<bit> unsigned height, then a one-<bit> mode: 0 means raw <pixels>, 1 means <run-length encoding>. Fields are written most significant <bit> first; this profile accepts only width and height 8, so both decoders know to stop at 64 <pixels>. Raw mode contains 64 <bits>. <RLE> mode contains the initial <bit>, followed by seven-<bit> unsigned run lengths in the range 1 to 64. Zero and lengths above 64 are invalid; a decoder rejects a run that would exceed the remaining <pixel> count. It stops when the decoded lengths sum to 64, so no run-count field or end marker is needed.

For this image, the complete <RLE> serialization is:
```
00001000 00001000 1 | 0 | 0001100 0001000 0001000 0001000 0001000 0001000 0001100
```
Spaces and vertical bars are explanatory separators, not stored characters. The seven-<bit> fields directly store the lengths; 64 would be `1000000`. The payload costs $1+7\cdot7=50$ <bits>, versus 64 raw <bits>. The 17-<bit> <file header> has the same size in either mode, giving complete sizes of 67 versus 81 <bits>. If packed into eight-<bit> units, append zero padding only at the end: the sizes become 72 versus 88 <bits>. These are sizes of this toy representation, not sizes of <PNG> files.

The field design matters. Restarting <RLE> on every row costs eight initial <bits> plus four <bits> per run when lengths are stored directly: three <bits> represent only 0 through 7, whereas a row can have length 8. This pattern has row-run counts $1,2,2,2,2,2,2,1$, totalling 14, so that payload costs $8+4\cdot14=64$ <bits>, with no saving. Storing length minus one would instead fit lengths 1 through 8 into three <bits>, but is a different declared <file format>. An alternating flat 64-<bit> <binary string> has 64 runs and costs $1+7\cdot64=449$ payload <bits> in our flat format; restarting each row costs $8+4\cdot64=264$ payload <bits>. A one-run image costs only eight payload <bits>. These extremes explain when <RLE> is useful: long uninterrupted regions make short instructions, while frequent changes make instructions expensive.

<Prefix codes> of unequal lengths can replace fixed-width run lengths, provided the decoder knows the code and any necessary code description is counted. <Run-length encoding> names a technique, not one universal serialization. For a real implementation, Microsoft's bitmap documentation specifies `BI_RLE8` with count and value fields plus special commands, rather than our initial-<bit> convention.https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/gdi/bitmap-compression[Microsoft, Bitmap Compression, BI_RLE8 encoded and absolute modes]{ref}

\Image[run-length-image-compression.png]
{title=Seven runs serialize an 8 by 8 binary image and reconstruct every pixel exactly}

= Deflate
{c}
{parent=Data compression}
{wiki}

<Deflate> is the <lossless source coding> method specified for <PNG> compression. It can describe repeated stretches of earlier data, going beyond the single-value repetitions of <run-length encoding>.<Portable Network Graphics (PNG) Specification (Third Edition) by Mark Adler and others>[W3C, PNG Specification, Third Edition, section 10.1, compression method 0]{ref}

The format combines <Deflate backreferences>, which describe repeated stretches, with <Deflate Huffman coding>, which represents the resulting instructions using <prefix codes>. <Deflate block framing> tells the decoder which codes apply and where decoding stops. RFC 1951, https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc1951[DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3] (May 1996), defines the format; different encoders can choose different instructions for the same <input>.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, heading and sections 2 and 4: format and encoder choices]{ref}

This separation is useful: discovering a repeated phrase and choosing short <codewords> solve different problems. A repeated phrase can contain many distinct <bytes>, while unequal <codeword lengths> can help even when no phrase repeats. Neither mechanism removes <Deflate compression overhead> or guarantees a saving on every <input>.

= Deflate literal byte
{c}
{parent=Deflate}

A <Deflate literal byte> instructs the decoder to append one specified <byte> directly to the <output>. For example, three <Deflate literal bytes> with values 65, 66 and 67 produce `ABC`. These values are symbols in the <Deflate Huffman coding> stage, rather than necessarily eight raw stored <bits> each. A <Deflate backreference> instead describes a copy from earlier <output>.

= Deflate backreference
{c}
{parent=Deflate}
{title2=$(\ell,d)$}

= Deflate length-distance pair
{c}
{synonym}

A <Deflate backreference> specifies a length $\ell$ and backward distance $d$, measured in decoded <bytes>. If $n$ <bytes> have already been produced, append $\ell$ <bytes> starting at <output> index $n-d$, counting indices from zero. The permitted ranges are $3\leq\ell\leq258$ and $1\leq d\leq32768$. Without a supplied initial history, $d\leq n$ is also required. The distance refers to decoded history, not a position in the compressed <input>.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, sections 3.2.3 and 3.2.5: available history, overlap, length and distance ranges]{ref}

For the twelve <bytes> `ABCABCABCABC`, an illustrative instruction sequence is three <Deflate literal bytes> `A`, `B`, `C`, followed by the <Deflate backreference> $(\ell,d)=(9,3)$. <Run-length encoding> sees twelve single-<byte> runs because neighbouring values differ; the <Deflate backreference> describes a repeated three-<byte> pattern instead. Conversely, a <Deflate literal byte> `A` followed by $(7,1)$ produces `AAAAAAAA`: distance one can express a single-value repetition. These are chosen instructional examples, not claims about an encoder's chosen instructions or complete serialized streams.

= Deflate overlapping copy
{c}
{parent=Deflate backreference}

In a <Deflate overlapping copy>, $\ell>d$: some source <bytes> are created by the very copy being decoded. For each step $i=0,\ldots,\ell-1$, read the current <output> at index $n+i-d$ and append that <byte> at index $n+i$. Because $d\geq1$, the source index is always smaller than the destination index, so the needed <byte> exists when it is read.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, section 3.2.3, printed p. 11: overlapping string references]{ref}

For `ABC` followed by $(9,3)$, the source indices are $0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8$ and the destination indices are $3,4,5,6,7,8,9,10,11$. Steps 0 through 2 copy the original `ABC`; steps 3 through 5 copy the `ABC` just appended; steps 6 through 8 copy the next newly appended `ABC`. Thus the final <output> is exactly `ABCABCABCABC`. Taking a snapshot of the original three-<byte> source slice would produce only three <bytes>; that operation does not implement the specified nine-<byte> copy.

\Image[deflate-overlapping-copy-trace.png]
{title=Nine successive copies at distance three extend ABC to ABCABCABCABC using newly produced bytes}

The reusable idea is a short description of periodic continuation: the decoder repeatedly looks a fixed distance behind its growing <output>. A <Deflate backreference> can also reach across a <Deflate block framing> boundary. Changing the block's <Huffman codes> does not by itself erase the decoded history.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, sections 2 and 3.2.3: independent block codes, references across blocks]{ref}

= Deflate Huffman coding
{c}
{parent=Deflate}

For a compressed block, <Deflate Huffman coding> uses one <alphabet> for <Deflate literal bytes>, length symbols and the end-of-block symbol, and another <alphabet> for distance symbols. Values 0 through 255 denote <Deflate literal bytes>, 256 ends the block, and 257 through 285 select lengths. A <Deflate backreference> is encoded as a length symbol, any length extra <bits>, a distance symbol, then any distance extra <bits>. Those extra <bits> select an offset within the symbol's prescribed range; they are not additional <codewords>.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, section 3.2.5, length and distance tables]{ref}

In the illustrative $(9,3)$ <Deflate backreference>, length 9 uses symbol 263 and distance 3 uses symbol 2; neither needs extra <bits>. Under the fixed <Huffman codes>, `A`, `B` and `C` each cost eight <bits>, symbol 263 costs seven, and distance symbol 2 costs five. Adding the seven-<bit> end-of-block symbol and three-<bit> block header gives $3+3\cdot8+7+5+7=46$ <bits>, packed into six <bytes> for a single final block. This is a cost calculation for the explicitly chosen instructions, not a displayed compressed stream.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, sections 3.2.5 and 3.2.6: length 9, distance 3 and fixed code lengths]{ref}

Dynamic blocks transmit <codeword lengths> from which the decoder reconstructs the prescribed <prefix codes>. The code descriptions themselves use a smaller <Huffman code> and repeat instructions for successive <codeword lengths>, including runs of zero lengths. Here <run-length encoding> describes repeated code lengths, a separate role from copying repeated data <bytes>. Fixed blocks use predefined <Huffman codes> and avoid transmitting those descriptions.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, sections 3.2.2, 3.2.6 and 3.2.7: code reconstruction, fixed codes and dynamic code-length repeats]{ref}

= Deflate block framing
{c}
{parent=Deflate}

Each block starts with a one-<bit> `BFINAL` flag and a two-<bit> `BTYPE` field. The `BTYPE` values are 0 for stored data, 1 for fixed <Huffman codes>, 2 for dynamic <Huffman codes>, and 3 for an invalid reserved value. For compressed blocks, symbol 256 ends the current block; `BFINAL = 1` says this is the last block. An end-of-block symbol does not mean a literal zero <byte>, and in a nonfinal block it does not end the whole stream.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, section 3.2.3: header, block types and decoding loop]{ref}

\Image[deflate-token-block-pipeline.png]
{title=Deflate separates literal and copy instructions from Huffman coding and final-block framing; stored blocks bypass both stages}

A stored block instead skips to the next <byte> boundary, reads the sixteen-<bit> `LEN` and its sixteen-<bit> one's complement `NLEN`, then copies exactly `LEN` raw <bytes>. Its length field replaces the compressed block's end-of-block symbol. A compressed block need not end at a <byte> boundary, so the next block header can start inside the current <byte>.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, sections 3.2.3 and 3.2.4: alignment, LEN, NLEN and stored data]{ref}

The diagram shows logical fields, not a serialization into <bits>. RFC 1951 specifies <bit> packing separately, including different conventions for <codewords> and other fields (section 3.1.1). For <PNG>, the filtered image data is compressed inside a zlib wrapper with its own header and checksum; raw <Deflate> and a complete <PNG> file therefore have different framing.<Portable Network Graphics (PNG) Specification (Third Edition) by Mark Adler and others>[PNG Specification, Third Edition, sections 10.1 and 10.2: zlib wrapper and filtered image data]{ref}

= Deflate compression overhead
{c}
{parent=Deflate}

Short <inputs> can spend much of their size on <Deflate block framing>, the end-of-block symbol and, for dynamic blocks, <Deflate Huffman coding> descriptions. A repeated phrase is useful only if its <Deflate backreference> costs less than the <Deflate literal bytes> it replaces. <Huffman coding> adapts instruction costs; it does not make either references or code descriptions free.

For an exact expansion example, take the 256 <bytes> with values $0,1,\ldots,255$, each appearing once. A single fixed block containing only <Deflate literal bytes> costs $144\cdot8+112\cdot9+7+3=2170$ <bits>, or 272 <bytes> after final packing. One stored block starting at a <byte> boundary instead costs five overhead <bytes> plus the 256 data <bytes>, or 261 <bytes>. Both exceed the 256-<byte> <input>; these counts follow the specified layouts and are not measurements of an encoder's automatic choice. The example illustrates <universal lossless shortening is impossible>.<DEFLATE Compressed Data Format Specification version 1.3 by L. Peter Deutsch>[RFC 1951, sections 3.2.3, 3.2.4 and 3.2.6: header, stored layout and fixed literal lengths]{ref}

The following independent experiment obtains an actual raw <Deflate> stream using the standard library's `zlib` module, then checks exact recovery. Negative `wbits` selects raw framing; the three lines of compressed-data handling make no assumptions about which <Deflate backreferences> the library selects.<Python 3.14 documentation/zlib — Compression compatible with gzip (Python 3.14 documentation)>[Python 3.14 zlib documentation, compressobj, decompress and negative wbits]{ref}
```
import zlib
data = b"ABCABCABCABC"
encoder = zlib.compressobj(level=6, wbits=-15)
packed = encoder.compress(data) + encoder.flush()
assert zlib.decompress(packed, wbits=-15) == data
print(len(data), len(packed), packed.hex())
```
The printed <bytes> describe this library run. Exact roundtrip recovery is the check; a particular compressed length or instruction choice is not a guarantee for other <inputs> or library versions.

= Image compression
{parent=Data compression}
{wiki}

<Image compression> applies <data compression> to pictures, usually represented by <raster images>. Nearby <pixels> often have related shades; an encoder can exploit that structure. A flat region invites <run-length encoding>, while <lossy block averaging> trades fine detail for fewer stored samples. The choice depends on whether exact <pixel> values matter and on the patterns in the image.

<Lossless source coding> recovers the encoded <pixel> values exactly, including sharp text or numerical image data. <Lossy compression> preserves an approximation and can remove features that later <image processing> might need. A visually smooth result is therefore a different promise from exact recovery. Neither method has a universal compression ratio: the chosen <file format>, its <file header> and the particular image all matter.

<PNG> is a real format with <lossless source coding> of its encoded image data. Its specified compression uses <Deflate> on reversibly filtered rows, rather than our toy <RLE> format. The <lossy block averaging> example is also a teaching format, not a description of <PNG>. A lossy preprocessing step followed by saving as <PNG> preserves the already changed <pixels>, not the earlier original.<Portable Network Graphics (PNG) Specification (Third Edition) by Mark Adler and others>[W3C, PNG Specification, Third Edition (2025), section 10, Compression]{ref}

= PNG
{c}
{parent=Image compression}
{wiki}

= Portable Network Graphics
{c}
{synonym}

<PNG> is a <file format> for <raster images> with <lossless source coding> of the encoded image data. Its first W3C Recommendation, dated 1 October 1996, described a portable replacement for earlier image formats; this situates the design in exchanging images reliably between systems.https://www.w3.org/TR/REC-png-961001[W3C, PNG (Portable Network Graphics) Specification, 1 October 1996, status and abstract]{ref}

= Software module
{parent=Computer science}

A <software module> groups responsibilities behind an <application programming interface>. Its boundary can be maintained by procedures and hidden storage; it need not be a <class in object-oriented programming>. <Information hiding> guides which implementation decisions belong inside that boundary.

= Application programming interface
{parent=Software module}

= API
{c}
{synonym}

An <application programming interface> defines the operations available to client code and their promised behavior, including <inputs>, <outputs> and failure cases. For a <FIFO queue>, operation names alone do not specify what happens on an empty or full queue. An <API> should state those cases before clients depend on them.

= Encapsulation
{disambiguate=computer programming}
{parent=Computer science}
{wiki}

<Encapsulation (computer programming)> bundles related program state and operations or hides implementation details behind an interface. <Encapsulation in object-oriented programming> realizes this through objects and their permitted operations; encapsulation also applies to modules outside object-oriented programs.

The useful boundary is a promise: clients request operations through an <API>, and the implementation maintains a <representation invariant>. Instead of proving that every client updates several storage fields consistently, prove that the permitted operations preserve the <representation invariant>. <Information hiding> also leaves room to replace the <data structure> while keeping the client-facing behavior, a property called <representation independence>.

Bundling state and operations alone does not establish that promise. <Representation exposure> can let clients bypass the <API>. A `private` annotation alone is not a security guarantee: it does not establish correctness of operations, isolation from hostile code or safe handling of shared mutable storage. The examples below concern sequential calls with no concurrent access and no client access to internal storage; they do not claim protection against arbitrary memory modification.

= Information hiding
{parent=Encapsulation (computer programming)}

<Information hiding> makes an implementation decision the responsibility of one <software module>, whose <API> lets other code work without knowing that decision. For a <FIFO queue>, clients need the removal order and failure behavior; they need not know the current front index in an <array>. This explains why <encapsulation (computer programming)> is useful beyond <object-oriented programming>: procedures with hidden storage can provide the same boundary.<On the Criteria To Be Used in Decomposing Systems into Modules by D. L. Parnas>[Parnas, pp. 1054 and 1056, What Is Modularization? and The Criteria: responsibility assignments and hidden design decisions]{ref}

In <On the Criteria To Be Used in Decomposing Systems into Modules by D. L. Parnas> (December 1972, pp. 1054–1056), the example applies a <sorting algorithm> to word rotations of <input> lines. One decomposition shares storage layouts among processing stages. The alternative exposes line-storage operations such as `CHAR` and `SETCHAR`, hiding whether characters are packed four to a machine word. Changing that packing then belongs to line storage rather than every client. The paper presents a design argument using a deliberately small example, not a universal measured speedup or a claim that classes are required. It also notes that an unnecessarily specified rotation order restricts future implementations (p. 1056, Improvement in Circular Shift Module).

= Abstract data type
{parent=Encapsulation (computer programming)}
{wiki}

An <abstract data type> specifies values and operations independently of their storage. A <FIFO queue> has ordered <sequences> as its abstract values; an <array> and a <linked list> can represent those same values. A <representation invariant> identifies valid storage states, and an <abstraction function> says what each valid state means.<6.005 Software Construction, Spring 2016/Reading 13: Abstraction Functions & Rep Invariants>[Reading 13, Rep Invariant and Abstraction Function: representation values, abstract values, AF and RI]{ref}

= Representation invariant
{parent=Abstract data type}
{title2=$I(r)$}

A <representation invariant> $I(r)$ specifies which concrete storage states $r$ are valid at the <API> boundary. Construction must establish it, and each completed operation must preserve it. Internal steps may temporarily violate it only while that state is inaccessible to observers; callbacks or concurrent access would require further reasoning.<6.005 Software Construction, Spring 2016/Reading 13: Abstraction Functions & Rep Invariants>[Reading 13, How to Establish Invariants: creation, preservation and absence of representation exposure]{ref}

The short proof pattern is <mathematical induction> on the number of completed calls: the initial state satisfies $I$; every permitted transition from an $I$-state ends in an $I$-state; hence every state observable through the <API> satisfies $I$. An unrestricted external write falls outside this argument.

= Abstraction function
{parent=Abstract data type}
{title2=$\alpha:R_{\mathrm{valid}}\to Q$}

An <abstraction function> maps a valid concrete representation to its abstract value. Write $R_{\mathrm{valid}}=\{r:I(r)\}$ and $\alpha:R_{\mathrm{valid}}\to Q$. Different storage states can denote the same abstract value. Correct implementation requires both preservation of the <representation invariant> and agreement with the specified operation after applying the <abstraction function>; preserving a length bound alone would not prove first-in, first-out behavior.

= Bounded queue with encapsulation
{parent=Encapsulation (computer programming)}
{title2=$0\leq |q|\leq C$}

Consider an original <FIFO queue> example storing immutable labels, with fixed positive <integer> capacity $C$. Its abstract state is a finite <sequence> $q=(x_0,\ldots,x_{n-1})$, with $0\leq n\leq C$. The <API> has `enqueue(x)`, `dequeue()`, `peek()` and `size()`. Successful `enqueue` returns `ok`; a full queue returns `full` and is unchanged. Successful `dequeue` returns the first label and removes it; an empty queue returns `empty` and is unchanged. `peek` returns the first label or `empty` without modification, and `size` returns $n$. The status results are distinct from stored labels.

For a compact <array> representation $(A,n)$, fix $|A|=C$ and require the <representation invariant>
$$
I(A,n):\quad n\in\mathbb Z,\qquad 0\leq n\leq C.
$$
The <abstraction function> is $\alpha(A,n)=(A_0,\ldots,A_{n-1})$. Only these first $n$ entries matter; unused slots have no abstract meaning. On successful `enqueue(x)`, write $A_n=x$ and then set $n\leftarrow n+1$. On successful `dequeue()`, save $A_0$, shift the remaining $n-1$ entries left, and decrement $n$. Testing the full or empty case before updating gives the specified unchanged failure state.

\Image[encapsulated-queue-api-state-boundary.png]
{title=Permitted queue calls preserve valid states; an unrestricted count write bypasses the API}

With $C=3$ and $q=(A,B)$, `enqueue(C)` produces $(A,B,C)$; `enqueue(D)` returns `full` without change. A raw write $n\leftarrow4$ violates the <representation invariant>. More subtly, changing the count from two to one satisfies its bounds but discards $B$ without a permitted operation. Validity of the storage state and legality of the <API> transition are different obligations. A setter for every storage field would surrender the useful boundary even if its name looked like an ordinary operation.

= Representation exposure
{parent=Encapsulation (computer programming)}

<Representation exposure> gives outside code access that can modify storage on which the <representation invariant> or promised behavior depends. Returning the internal <array> exposes a route around the <API>; merely hiding the field name does not close it. Incoming shared mutable storage can create the same problem. Copies or immutable values can prevent such modification, but their costs and sharing rules belong in the design.<6.005 Software Construction, Spring 2016/Reading 13: Abstraction Functions & Rep Invariants>[Reading 13, Invariants, Immutability: mutable aliases through both returned values and constructor arguments]{ref}

The <bounded queue with encapsulation> deliberately stores immutable labels. A <FIFO queue> of mutable objects needs a separate promise about whether changing a returned object changes stored content; protecting the queue's ordering and size does not automatically make every stored object immutable.

= Representation independence
{parent=Encapsulation (computer programming)}

<Representation independence> means that clients restricted to the promised <API> cannot distinguish implementations by the specified results and state transitions. State what observations count: equal returned labels and failure statuses do not imply equal running times, allocations or physical layouts. If the <API> also promises a resource bound, a replacement must meet that bound too.

Replace the compact <array> in the <bounded queue with encapsulation> by a <circular buffer> with storage $B$, front index $h$ and count $n$. Its <representation invariant> is $|B|=C$, $h,n\in\mathbb Z$, $0\leq h<C$ and $0\leq n\leq C$. Its <abstraction function> reads the occupied slots using <modular arithmetic>:
$$
\alpha(B,h,n)=\bigl(B_{(h+i)\bmod C}\bigr)_{i=0}^{n-1}.
$$
On successful `enqueue(x)`, write $B_{(h+n)\bmod C}=x$ and increment $n$. On successful `dequeue()`, return $B_h$, replace $h$ by $(h+1)\bmod C$ and decrement $n$. Full and empty failures do not alter storage. `peek` and `size` have the same abstract meanings as before. Keeping a count distinguishes a full <circular buffer> from an empty one even when both would have the same next insertion index.

\Image[queue-array-ring-representation-trace.png]
{title=The same bounded queue trace uses shifting array storage or a wrapping circular buffer}

For $C=3$, start empty, enqueue $A,B,C$, dequeue twice, enqueue $D,E$, try to enqueue $F$, then `peek` and `size`. Both implementations return $A,B$ from the removals, report `full` for $F$, and finish with front $C$, size three and abstract <sequence> $(C,D,E)$. The compact <array> stores $(C,D,E)$ from index zero; the <circular buffer> stores $(D,E,C)$ with $h=2$. Their <abstraction functions> explain why different stored entries describe the same ordered content.

Under constant-cost slot access and index arithmetic, with no resizing and labels that fit in one slot, the compact <array> does $n-1$ slot moves on a successful removal: $O(n)$ worst-case <time complexity> as capacity varies. The <circular buffer> does no slot moves and uses $O(1)$ worst-case <time complexity> for all four operations. Both allocate $C$ slots initially; that construction cost is separate. These are operation-count claims in <Big O notation>, not measured timings. The short correctness outline checks that each implementation's <abstraction function> produces the specified new <sequence> and return value after every successful or rejected call. Repeating that check gives equal traces for clients using only this <API>.

= Hash function
{parent=Computer science}
{wiki}
{title2=$h:U\to B$}

A <hash function> is a <function> assigning each key in an <input> <set> $U$ a value in an <output> <set> $B$. Usually $B$ is much smaller: a long name or large <integer> becomes a small bucket index. Equal keys must receive equal values from the same <hash function>; different keys may also receive equal values. Such a <hash collision> means "look in the same place", and does not establish key equality. If $|U|>|B|$, the <pigeonhole principle> makes some collisions unavoidable.

For example, <modular arithmetic> gives the <hash function> $h(k)=k\bmod7$, with seven bucket indices $0,\ldots,6$. Store the seven keys $0,2,3,10,11,17,24$: their values are respectively $0,2,3,3,4,3,3$. In particular, $h(3)=h(10)=3$ although $3\ne10$. A <hash table> is a <data structure> that uses these indices to locate records. With <separate chaining>, each bucket holds a <linked list> of records, so bucket $3$ retains all four keys $3,10,17,24$. Inserting $10$ must not overwrite $3$ merely because their <hash function> values agree.

\Image[hash-bucket-collisions.png]
{title=Seven keys stored in seven buckets using the hash function h(k) = k mod 7}
{description=Orange arrows identify four colliding keys. Bucket 3 keeps all four records in a linked list; equality is checked on the stored keys.}

Searching for $10$ computes bucket $3$, then compares the actual keys until it reaches $10$. Searching for absent key $31$ reaches that same bucket and must reject every stored key. This <data structure> trades a search through all records for a search through one short chain, provided the <hash function> distributes keys adequately. A fixed <hash function> can perform poorly on patterned keys: $3,10,17,24$ already demonstrate the problem. A <universal hash family> introduces controlled randomness to limit the <expected value> of this extra work. Neither indexing nor a small collision <probability> inherently supplies <encryption> or <message authentication>; a <cryptographic hash function> has additional requirements such as <collision resistance>.

= Hash collision
{parent=Hash function}
{title2=$x\ne y,\ h(x)=h(y)$}

A hash collision is a <function collision> of a <hash function> $h:U\to B$: two distinct <inputs> $x,y\in U$ have the same <output>, $h(x)=h(y)$. Repeating the same <input> is not a <hash collision>. Unequal hash values prove the keys differ; equal hash values only identify candidates that might be equal. This makes a <hash function> useful as a compact filter before comparing the original data.

Here is a deliberately simple example using <modular arithmetic>. Let $h(k)=k\bmod16$ for nonnegative <integer> keys, and let a four-bucket <hash table> use the further bucket index $b(k)=h(k)\bmod4$. For keys $2,6,18$, the hash values are $2,6,2$, while all three bucket indices are $2$. Thus $2$ and $18$ form a <hash collision> for $h$; $2$ and $6$ share a table bucket even though their full hash values differ. Always specify which <function> is being compared: a table collision concerns the bucket-index <function>, which may discard more information than the original <hash function>.

\Image[hash-collision-key-identity.png]
{title=A shared hash value and a shared table bucket discard different distinctions}
{description=Constructed <integer> keys $2,6,18$ pass through $h(k)=k\bmod16$ and $b(k)=h(k)\bmod4$. Orange highlights the <hash collision> between $2$ and $18$; blue key $6$ joins them only after bucket reduction. <Separate chaining> retains all three keys. This is a toy calculation, not a measured workload or a <cryptographic hash function>.}

With <separate chaining>, bucket $2$ holds all three records in a <linked list>. A search for $18$ visits that bucket and compares the stored keys until it finds $18$; a search for $34$ visits the same bucket but rejects every stored key. A <hash collision> changes the search work, while key comparison preserves correctness. Storing only one record per hash value would incorrectly merge distinct keys. This is the purpose of collision handling in a <hash table>.<6.006 Introduction to Algorithms, Fall 2011 by Massachusetts Institute of Technology, 6.006 course staff/Lecture 8: Hashing I>{ref}

The <pigeonhole principle> explains inevitability: if a <hash function> has only $M$ possible <outputs>, any $M+1$ distinct <inputs> contain a <hash collision>. In particular, a <function> whose <domain of a function> is larger than its finite <codomain> cannot be an <injective function>. This is an existence statement; it gives neither an efficient way to find the pair nor a <probability> for a smaller collection of keys. A particular small collection might have no <hash collision> at all.

For a <cryptographic hash function>, <collision resistance> asks that finding any two distinct messages with equal full digests be computationally infeasible under the specified model and resources. It does not mean that <hash collisions> do not exist. <Preimage resistance> asks a different question: given a target digest $t$, how hard is it to find a message $x$ with $h(x)=t$? The collision finder may choose both messages and their common digest; the <preimage> finder is given the digest in advance. These definitions distinguish two search tasks, independently of how a <hash table> stores records.https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/SP/nistspecialpublication800-107r1.pdf[Quynh Dang, NIST SP 800-107 Revision 1 (2012), section 3.1 and section 4.1, definitions of collision and preimage resistance]{ref}

= Birthday probability for hash outputs
{parent=Hash collision}
{title2=$p_M(n)=1-\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-j/M)$}

How soon should a <hash collision> appear? Specify the <probability distribution> of the outputs first. Evaluate $n$ distinct keys and assume their hash values $Y_1,\ldots,Y_n$ are <independent random variables>, each with the <uniform distribution on a finite set> of $M\geq1$ possible <outputs>. The randomness may come from an idealized randomly chosen <hash function>; a fixed <hash function> and a fixed list of keys have a definite outcome. Sampling the same key twice creates a repeated hash value without creating a <hash collision>, so distinctness of the queried keys matters.

This is the existing <birthday problem>, with hash values in place of birthdays. Let $p_M(n)$ be the <probability> of at least one equal pair. The exact formula is
$$
p_M(n)=\begin{cases}
1-\displaystyle\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\left(1-\frac{j}{M}\right),&0\leq n\leq M,\\
1,&n>M.
\end{cases}
$$
The empty product is $1$, so $p_M(0)=p_M(1)=0$. Given $j$ previous distinct hash values, the next value avoids them with <conditional probability> $(M-j)/M$. Multiply these conditional chances and subtract from $1$. Equivalently, for $n\leq M$, exactly $M(M-1)\cdots(M-n+1)$ of the $M^n$ equally likely ordered outcomes have no repeated value. This derivation also explains why independence of all outputs, rather than merely uniform individual values, is required.https://cacr.uwaterloo.ca/hac/about/chap2.pdf[Menezes, van Oorschot and Vanstone, Handbook of Applied Cryptography, section 2.1.5, Fact 2.27(i), birthday probability]{ref}

\Image[hash-collision-birthday-versus-target.png]
{title=Any matching pair appears much sooner than a match to one fixed target}
{description=Computed <probabilities> for $M=256$ under the independent uniform hash-output model. Left: the exact <birthday problem> formula and its exponential approximation over the first 48 distinct queries. Right: any-pair collisions and hitting a digest fixed before the queries, over 256 distinct queries. Their first integer sample sizes reaching 50 percent are $20$ and $178$. These are theoretical toy curves, not measurements of a real <cryptographic hash function>.}

For $M=256$, the exact <hash collision> <probabilities> are $p_{256}(19)\approx0.495742$, $p_{256}(20)\approx0.533167$, and $p_{256}(32)\approx0.867643$. Thus only $20$ distinct queries already exceed a one-half chance of some pair matching. The striking feature is the number of opportunities: $n$ queries supply $\binom n2$ pairs. By <linearity of expectation>, the <expected value> of the number of equal pairs is $\lambda=\binom n2/M$. This expectation counts pairs, not the <probability> of at least one pair; three equal outputs contribute three pairs in one outcome. The <union bound> gives $p_M(n)\leq\min(1,\lambda)$.

Using the <natural logarithm> expansion $\log(1-u)=-u+O(u^2)$ in the exact product gives
$$
p_M(n)\approx1-\exp\left(-\frac{n(n-1)}{2M}\right).
$$
For $n\leq M/2$, the omitted correction in the logarithm of the no-collision <probability> is $O(n^3/M^2)$. Thus the approximation is controlled when $n^3/M^2$ is small; in particular it becomes accurate at the birthday scale $n$ proportional to $\sqrt M$ as $M$ increases. The inequality $1-u\leq e^{-u}$ also shows that the displayed approximation is a lower bound for $p_M(n)$ when $n\leq M$. Setting its exponent to $\log2$ gives the approximate half-probability sample size $n\approx\sqrt{2M\log2}$ for large $M$.

For comparison, under the same independent uniform model the <probability> of hitting one previously fixed digest $t$ in $n$ queries is
$$
q_M(n)=1-\left(1-\frac1M\right)^n.
$$
Each query supplies one chance to hit $t$, instead of a growing number of possible pairs. For $M=256$, $q_{256}(20)\approx0.075293$, and the first integer $n$ with $q_{256}(n)\geq1/2$ is $178$. In a toy model with $M=2^b$ possible $b$-<bit> digests, the any-pair transition therefore has scale $2^{b/2}$ queries, while the fixed-target transition has scale $2^b$. This explains the birthday-search distinction between <collision resistance> and <preimage resistance>.https://cacr.uwaterloo.ca/hac/about/chap9.pdf[Menezes, van Oorschot and Vanstone, Handbook of Applied Cryptography, Fact 9.33 and section 9.7.1, generic collision versus preimage searches]{ref}

The assumptions are essential. Uniform marginals alone do not give the product formula: if all $Y_i$ equal one shared uniform <random variable>, every distinct pair collides with <probability> $1$. A <universal hash family> bounds each pair's <hash collision> <probability>, but that condition alone does not supply joint independence of every output. Nor does a fixed output length establish <collision resistance> or <preimage resistance>: the toy $h(k)=k\bmod16$ has $h(k)=h(k+16)$, making deliberate <hash collisions> immediate. The curves describe the specified model and do not establish the security of a concrete <cryptographic hash function>.

= Almost strongly universal hash family
{parent=Hash function}
{title2=$\Pr_h[h(x)=u,h(y)=v]\leq\varepsilon/q$}

= Almost strongly universal2 hashing
{synonym}

An <almost strongly universal hash family> relaxes the exact <ordered pair> balance of a <strongly universal hash family>. In the convention used here, choose $h$ with the <uniform distribution on a finite set> from a family with $q$ possible <outputs>. Every individual <output> remains uniform, and for every distinct pair of <inputs> $x,y$ and every specified <ordered pair> of <outputs> $u,v$,
$$
\Pr_h[h(x)=u,h(y)=v]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{q}.
$$
Equivalently, the <conditional probability> $\Pr_h[h(y)=v\mid h(x)=u]$ is at most $\varepsilon$. This asks how predictable a second <output> can become after the first is known.

For $q=5$, <strongly universal hash families> give $\varepsilon=1/5$: learning one <output> leaves each specified second <output> with <conditional probability> $1/5$. A looser guarantee $\varepsilon=2/5$ permits a specified <ordered pair> to have <probability> as large as $2/25$, while each individual <output> still has <probability> $1/5$. Summing over the five equal <ordered pairs> bounds the collision <probability> by $2/5$, rather than the $1/5$ required of a <universal hash family>. Thus an <almost strongly universal hash family> need not be a <universal hash family>. Necessarily $\varepsilon\geq1/q$, because the $q$ conditional <probabilities> sum to one; equality recovers a <strongly universal hash family>. This relaxation is useful in <Wegman–Carter authentication>, where efficiency can be traded against a quantified forgery bound within a complete <message authentication> protocol.

= Polynomial almost strongly universal hashing
{parent=Almost strongly universal hash family}
{title2=$h_{a,b}(m)=b+\sum_{j=1}^Lm_ja^j$}

For fixed-length messages in $\mathbb F_q^L$, choose independent uniform field elements $a,b$. Each output is uniform because $b$ masks it. For distinct messages and prescribed output difference $d$, the equation $\sum_{j=1}^L(m'_j-m_j)a^j=d$ is a nonzero polynomial equation of degree at most $L$, so the <root bound for a polynomial> gives at most $L$ choices of $a$. The joint output probability is at most $L/q^2$. Thus the family is $(L/q)$-almost strongly universal when $L<q$. Starting the message polynomial at power one matters: using a freely varying message coefficient at power zero would permit a known constant tag shift after observing one tag.

= Universal hash family
{parent=Hash function}
{title2=$\Pr_h[h(x)=h(y)]\leq1/q$}

= Universal2 hashing
{synonym}

A <universal hash family> is a finite collection $\mathcal H$ of <hash functions> into an <output> <set> of size $q$ such that, for any fixed distinct <inputs> $x,y$,
$$
\Pr_{h\in\mathcal H}[h(x)=h(y)]\leq\frac1q.
$$
The choice of $h$ uses the <uniform distribution on a finite set>. The keys are fixed before that choice: the randomness belongs to the <hash function>, so the guarantee does not assume naturally random keys. Choose one member and keep it while using the table; changing it requires redistributing the stored records. The bound holds separately for every distinct pair, without requiring uniform individual <outputs> or <independent random variables>. A <strongly universal hash family> supplies those stronger properties.

The practical payoff follows from <linearity of expectation>. If $n$ stored keys are distinct from a fixed query, let $C$ count how many share its bucket. Then $\mathbb E[C]\leq n/q$: each key contributes at most $1/q$ to the <expected value>. With $100$ stored keys and $100$ buckets, an unsuccessful search encounters at most one record on average over the random choice of <hash function>. This is an <expected value>, not a limit on every search; one unlucky choice can still create a long chain.

This calculation needs no <independence> between the collision events: <linearity of expectation> adds their individual <probabilities>. The fixed-query condition matters, since deliberately choosing keys after inspecting the selected <hash function> can target a crowded bucket.

Carter and Wegman introduced this way of analyzing storage and retrieval in their 1977 paper: average over a chosen class of <hash functions> while allowing any fixed sequence of <inputs>. Their 1979 journal version develops the same universal-pair collision condition and constructions. The insight is useful because a <randomized algorithm> can obtain a performance guarantee through its own choices, even when its <inputs> have inconvenient patterns.https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/800105.803400[Carter and Wegman, Universal classes of hash functions, STOC 1977]{ref}https://www.cs.princeton.edu/courses/archive/fall09/cos521/Handouts/universalclasses.pdf[Carter and Wegman, Universal Classes of Hash Functions, 1979]{ref}

= Strongly universal hash family
{parent=Universal hash family}
{title2=$\Pr_h[h(x)=u,h(y)=v]=q^{-2}$}

= Strongly universal2 hashing
{synonym}

A <strongly universal hash family> makes every <ordered pair> of <outputs> equally likely: for distinct <inputs> $x,y$ and arbitrary specified <outputs> $u,v$,
$$
\Pr_h[h(x)=u,h(y)=v]=\frac1{q^2}.
$$
Thus the two <outputs> are <independent random variables>, each with the <uniform distribution on a finite set>. Adding the $q$ equal <ordered pairs> gives collision <probability> $q/q^2=1/q$, so the family is also a <universal hash family>.

Here is a completely enumerable example. In the <finite field> $\mathbb F_5$, take the <affine functions> $h_{a,b}(x)=ax+b$, using <modular arithmetic> modulo $5$. Choose $a$ and $b$ as <independent random variables>, each with the <uniform distribution on a finite set> of all five <finite field> elements $0,1,2,3,4$, including $a=0$. There are $25$ equally likely <hash functions>. For $x=0,y=1$, the <ordered pair> is $(b,a+b)$. For instance $(a,b)=(2,3)$ gives $(h(0),h(1))=(3,0)$; $(0,3)$ gives $(3,3)$. Every prescribed $(u,v)$ occurs exactly once, at $b=u,a=v-u$.

\Image[affine-hash-pair-grid.png]
{title=Enumerating all affine hash functions over the finite field with five elements}
{description=Each cell shows the unique coefficient pair (a,b) producing the displayed outputs at 0 and 1. Removing a = 0 deletes the diagonal and leaves dependent outputs despite uniform individual values.}

Each row contains five choices, so each individual <output> has <probability> $5/25=1/5$. Each cell has <probability> $1/25$, and the five diagonal cells give collision <probability> $5/25=1/5$. For any distinct $x,y$, the same conclusion follows from $a=(v-u)/(y-x)$ and $b=u-ax$: a nonzero <finite field> element has a unique inverse. This is the beauty of the construction: two small random coefficients provide exact pair balance without choosing five unrelated values.

The example uses equal-sized <input> and <output> <sets> to make every choice visible; it illustrates the family definitions rather than compression. In a larger <input> <set>, a <hash function> cannot avoid all collisions into five buckets. What a <universal hash family> controls is the fraction of its members that collide on each fixed pair, rather than the existence of any collision anywhere.

Restricting to $a\ne0$ leaves $20$ <permutations> with zero collision <probability> for distinct <inputs>. Individual <outputs> remain uniform, but given $h(0)=u$, the four values other than $u$ each have <conditional probability> $1/4$ and $u$ has zero. Hence these <outputs> are not <independent random variables>: this is a <universal hash family> but not a <strongly universal hash family>. Finally, the full $25$-member example contains five constant <functions>, with immediately visible collisions. Its pair guarantee concerns a random choice of <hash function>, and makes no claim of cryptographic <collision resistance>.

= Conway Game of Life
{parent=Computer science}
{c}

= Conway's Game of Life
{c}
{synonym}

<Conway's Game of Life> is a <cellular automaton> in which a simple local rule produces patterns that remain still, pulse, or travel across a square grid. Choose the starting pattern; after that there are no player decisions. The <Life blinker> and <Life glider> below make the surprise concrete: the rule mentions only births and survival, yet repeated applications create a clock and a moving shape.

The standard board is infinite, with cells at <ordered pairs> $(x,y)\in\mathbb Z^2$ of <integers>. At generation $t\in\{0,1,2,\ldots\}$ let $a_t(x,y)$ be $1$ for a live cell and $0$ for a dead cell. Count all eight cells sharing an edge or a corner, excluding the center itself:
$$
n_t(x,y)=\sum_{\substack{i,j\in\{-1,0,1\}\\(i,j)\ne(0,0)}}a_t(x+i,y+j).
$$
The local <Boolean function> defining the next generation is
$$
a_{t+1}(x,y)=\begin{cases}1,&n_t(x,y)=3,\\1,&a_t(x,y)=1\text{ and }n_t(x,y)=2,\\0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
$$
This is the rule `B3/S23`: a dead cell is born with exactly three live neighbors; a live cell survives with exactly two or three. A live cell with zero or one, or with four through eight, dies. A dead cell with two stays dead. All counts and center states come from generation $t$: this is a synchronous update, even if an <algorithm> computes its cells one at a time.https://github.com/nathanieljohnston/game-of-life-book/blob/master/early_life.tex[Johnston and Greene, Conway's Game of Life: Mathematics and Construction, chapter 1: simultaneous updates and B3/S23 notation]{ref}

In particular, the two births in the <Life blinker> do not help any other cell survive until the following generation. Writing each answer over its old value and then reading that new value for another count changes the rule. The simplest correct <algorithm> reads one <array> and writes the next generation into a second <array>; swap their roles after the entire step. The <one-row in-place Life update> achieves the same rule with less auxiliary storage by preserving the old values that later counts still need.

A finite implementation must specify its boundary convention. Holding all cells outside the rectangle permanently dead can destroy a <Life glider> when it reaches the edge. Wrapping neighbor coordinates from one edge to the opposite edge allows interaction with patterns on the other side. Neither convention is the infinite board. The figures display finite windows into an infinite board initially dead everywhere except the stated live cells; the windows do not impose walls.
