Lookup computes the bucket index, then follows that chain until a <key equality> comparison succeeds or the chain ends. A <hash collision> therefore affects the search work without merging unequal keys. Deletion searches the same chain and unlinks the matching record; in a singly <linked list>, remembering the preceding record during the search permits the unlink.<Open Data Structures: An Introduction by Pat Morin/5.1 ChainedHashTable: Hashing with Chaining>{ref}

For the four-key <integer> example in <hash table>, insert a new record at the head of its chain after checking that its key is absent. Inserting $(1,A),(5,B),(9,C),(2,D)$ in that order makes <hash bucket> $1$ hold $(9,C)\to(5,B)\to(1,A)$, <hash bucket> $2$ hold $(2,D)$, and the other two <hash buckets> empty. All five subsequent operations below select <hash bucket> $1$ because $5\bmod4=13\bmod4=9\bmod4=1$:

\Image[hash-table-chain-operations.png]
{id=hash-table-chain-operations}
{title=<Separate chaining> traces lookup, replacement, deletion and insertion through <key equality>}
{description=Original deterministic model with $h_4(k)=k\bmod4$. The table counts actual keys inspected, not elapsed machine time. Operations occur from top to bottom; only an absent key adds a new record, and the untouched record $(2,D)$ remains in <hash bucket> $2$.}

`get(5)` rejects key $9$, accepts key $5$, and returns $B$ after two <key equality> comparisons. `get(13)` rejects $9,5,1$ and reports absence after three comparisons. `put(5,B*)` examines $9,5$ and replaces the value without changing $n=4$. `delete(9)` examines just the head record, removes it, and gives $n=3$. Finally, `put(13,E)` rejects $5,1$ before inserting the new head record; $n$ returns to $4$ and the chain is $(13,E)\to(5,B*)\to(1,A)$. The successive <hash table load factors> are $1,1,1,3/4,1$. These counts follow from the stipulated chain order; a different insertion policy changes the successful-search counts.

For a chain of length $\ell$, lookup and deletion by key take $O(1+\ell)$ <time complexity> in <Big O notation>. The same applies to `put` before any resizing, since replacement semantics require checking for an existing equal key. Head insertion alone is $O(1)$ if absence is already known; that is a different precondition. These bounds assume $O(1)$ bucket-index hashing, <array> access, <key equality> comparisons, allocation and link updates. A long <string> or large <integer> can make hashing or comparison depend on its encoded length, so counting record visits alone does not establish constant running time.

Under <simple uniform hashing>, each distinct key's bucket index is an independent <random variable> with a <uniform distribution on a finite set> of $m$ indices. The keys and query are fixed independently of this random assignment. Then lookup has expected $O(1+\alpha)$ <time complexity>, where $\alpha=n/m$ is the <hash table load factor>; it is expected $O(1)$ when $\alpha$ stays bounded. These are model assumptions, not an automatic consequence of using a deterministic <hash function>.<6.006 Introduction to Algorithms, Fall 2011 by Massachusetts Institute of Technology, 6.006 course staff/Lecture 8: Hashing I>[pp. 4–5, chaining worst case and simple uniform hashing assumption]{ref}

Here is the short calculation. For an absent query $q$ and stored keys $k_1,\ldots,k_n$, let $I_i$ be the <indicator random variable> of $h_m(k_i)=h_m(q)$. The number $C$ of records inspected has <expected value>
$$
C=\sum_{i=1}^{n}I_i,\qquad \mathbb E[C]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb E[I_i]=\frac{n}{m}=\alpha,
$$
by <linearity of expectation>. For a present fixed query, scanning its entire chain inspects at most itself plus all other colliding records, with <expected value> $1+(n-1)/m$; stopping at the match only decreases this upper bound. Adding constant hashing and bucket access yields expected $O(1+\alpha)$ for either query. The argument needs pairwise collision bounds; it does not require all the <indicator random variables> to be independent. A <universal hash family> can provide a related bound by random choice of a <hash function> for a fixed key set, as explained in that article.

The worst case remains $O(n)$, and an absent query takes $n$ comparisons if all $n$ records occupy its <hash bucket>. For instance, $1,1+m,1+2m,\ldots,1+(n-1)m$ all collide under $h_m(k)=k\bmod m$. <Separate chaining> remains correct, but a <sequence> of insertions that each checks the existing chain can take quadratic total work. <Amortized analysis> of resizing does not remove this search cost, and an adaptive choice of keys based on a known <hash function> is outside the stated <simple uniform hashing> model.

= Simple uniform hashing
{parent=Separate chaining}

A probabilistic model assigning distinct keys independent bucket indices with a <uniform distribution on a finite set>. It supplies an <expected value> bound for <separate chaining>, not a guarantee for every deterministic <hash function> or workload.

= Hash bucket
{parent=Hash table}

The collection of records assigned to one bucket index by a <hash function>. In <separate chaining>, an <array> slot refers to this collection, which may be empty.

= Key equality
{parent=Hash table}
{title2=$x\sim y$}

The <equivalence relation> used to decide whether two keys name the same record. A <hash function> must respect <key equality>; a <hash collision> does not establish <key equality>.

= Load factor of a hash table
{parent=Hash table}
{title2=$\alpha=n/m$}

= Hash table load factor
{synonym}

The number $n$ of stored records divided by the number $m>0$ of <hash buckets>. For <separate chaining>, this is the mean chain length over all <hash buckets>, including empty ones.

= Hash table resizing
{parent=Hash table}

Changing the number of <hash buckets> and reassigning stored records using the new bucket-index <hash function>. <Amortized analysis> of rebuilding and <expected value> bounds for subsequent lookup address different costs.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Array
{disambiguate=data structure}
{parent=Data structure}

= Array
{synonym}

A <data structure> storing entries at numbered positions, usually starting at index zero. The fixed-capacity representation considered here puts equal-sized slots consecutively in <computer memory>. The number $n$ of occupied slots can be smaller than the capacity; unused slots provide room for later entries. An <array> of references stores consecutive references, while the objects they identify can be elsewhere. https://opendatastructures.org/ods-java/2_Array_Based_Lists.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, chapter 2: array-based lists]{ref}

Let the first slot have abstract address $b$ and each slot occupy $s$ address units. Then indexed access computes
$$
\operatorname{address}(i)=b+i\,s,\qquad 0\leq i<n.
$$
For toy values $b=100$, $s=4$, the slots for $A,B,C$ start at $100,104,108$; index $2$ goes directly to $C$ through $100+2\cdot4=108$. These are illustrative slot addresses, not observed allocation addresses. Under the word-RAM (random-access machine) model for <time complexity>, word-sized <arithmetic operations> and any addressed <computer memory> access have constant cost, and the addresses and entries fit in words. Thus one <array> access takes $O(1)$ time in <Big O notation>, independently of the index. This is the access assumption needed by <binary search>. https://opendatastructures.org/ods-java/1_4_Model_Computation.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, section 1.4: the word-RAM model]{ref}

\Image[array-versus-linked-list-layout.png]
{title=An <array> computes a slot address; a <linked list> follows links}

Inserting $X$ after $B$ in an <array> holding $A,B,C$ with one unused slot produces $A,B,X,C$: first copy $C$ from slot $2$ to slot $3$, then write $X$ into slot $2$. Writing $X$ first would lose $C$ unless it had been saved separately. More generally, insert at index $i$ by copying occupied slots from right to left, then fill the gap; with spare capacity this moves $n-i$ entries and takes $O(1+n-i)$ time in <Big O notation>, so insertion near the middle has $O(n)$ <time complexity>. Removing an entry closes the gap by shifting later entries left. https://opendatastructures.org/ods-java/2_1_ArrayStack_Fast_Stack_O.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, section 2.1.1: indexed access and shifts]{ref}

A fixed-capacity <array> cannot accept another entry when full. A resizable representation can allocate a larger <array> and copy the entries. Doubling capacity when full gives $O(1)$ append cost by <amortized analysis> over a <sequence> starting empty, but a single append that copies $n$ entries still has $O(n)$ worst-case <time complexity>. The outline is a <geometric series>: copied capacities $1,2,4,\ldots,2^k$ sum to $2^{k+1}-1$, less than twice the number of appends that triggered the last copy. Middle insertion still pays for its shifts. https://opendatastructures.org/ods-java/2_1_ArrayStack_Fast_Stack_O.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, sections 2.1.2–2.1.3: resizing and amortized costs]{ref}

= Row-major order
{parent=Array (data structure)}

= Row-major
{synonym}

Storage of a multidimensional <array> with the last index varying fastest in consecutive positions. In a two-dimensional <array>, each row is contiguous. <Row-major order> is a layout convention; a program can traverse that <array> by rows or by columns. See the explicit address calculation in <spatial locality>.

= Column-major order
{parent=Array (data structure)}

= Column-major
{synonym}

Storage of a multidimensional <array> with the first index varying fastest in consecutive positions. In a two-dimensional <array>, each column is contiguous. Traversing a <row-major> <array> by columns does not change its storage into <column-major order>.

= Sorted array
{parent=Array (data structure)}
{title2=$A_0\leq A_1\leq\cdots\leq A_{n-1}$}

An <array> whose entries appear in nondecreasing order under a specified <total order>. The displayed inequalities allow repeated values.

For example, $(2,5,5,9)$ is a <sorted array> of <integers>. Inserting $7$ at index $3$ gives $(2,5,5,7,9)$: with spare capacity, first shift $9$ one slot right. Appending $7$ would violate the <sorted array> property. Updating an entry also requires preserving the <total order>, or removing and reinserting it in a suitable position.

The useful combination is order plus direct indexed access: <binary search> eliminates a whole interval of candidates at each comparison, giving $O(\log n)$ search <time complexity> under constant-cost comparisons and the <array> cost model. A <sorted array> is therefore attractive for many lookups between changes, while keeping it sorted can still require $O(n)$ shifting work per insertion. A sorted <linked list> has the ordering information but does not supply the same constant-cost access to a middle index.

= Circular buffer
{parent=Data structure}

= Ring buffer
{synonym}

A <circular buffer> uses a fixed-capacity <array> with indices that wrap through <modular arithmetic>. A front index and an occupied count can represent a <FIFO queue> without shifting entries after removal. The <representation independence> example specifies a rejecting full-buffer policy; overwriting the oldest entry would define different behavior.

= FIFO queue
{parent=Data structure}
{c}

= Queue data structure
{synonym}

A <FIFO queue> is a <data structure> with first-in, first-out behavior: `enqueue` inserts at the rear, `dequeue` removes and returns the front, and `peek` inspects the front without removing it. Removal and inspection require a nonempty <FIFO queue>, or an explicitly defined empty-case result. Enqueue $A,B,C$ in that order, dequeue $A$, then enqueue $D$: the remaining <sequence> is $B,C,D$, with $B$ next. A <LIFO stack> would remove the most recently inserted item instead. A <FIFO queue> models arrival-order service and supplies the next <vertex> for <breadth-first search>; a stochastic service model belongs to <queueing theory>. https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/Queue.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms: Queue interface and implementation]{ref}

The <FIFO queue> interface specifies removal order, not <time complexity>. A <linked list> with both front and rear pointers supports `enqueue`, `dequeue` and `peek` in $O(1)$ worst-case time under constant-cost pointer operations and allocation, as in Sedgewick and Wayne's implementation. A plain <array> that deletes its first entry by shifting the remaining $n-1$ entries takes $O(n)$ time per such removal. An <array> with advancing front and rear indices can avoid these shifts; storage and resizing policies still need analysis. The <two-list functional queue> gives a different implementation with constant <amortized analysis> cost along a single sequence of uses. These representation choices matter when deriving the linear <time complexity> of <breadth-first search> in <Big O notation>.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Two-list functional queue
{parent=FIFO queue}

Represent the abstract sequence by `front @ rev rear`. Keep the front nonempty unless both lists are empty. Prepending to the rear enqueues; when the front is exhausted, reverse the rear once. Along a single sequence of uses, each element crosses between lists at most once, giving constant <amortized analysis> cost per operation. Reusing an old persistent version can repeat a reversal and needs a separate analysis.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Stack
{disambiguate=data structure}
{parent=Data structure}
{wiki=Stack_(abstract_data_type)}

= Stack
{synonym}

= LIFO stack
{c}
{synonym}

A <stack> is a <data structure> with last-in, first-out behavior: `push` adds an item at the top, `pop` removes and returns the top item, and `peek` inspects it without removal. Push $A,B,C$: successive removals return $C,B,A$. Inspection and removal require a nonempty <stack>, or a specified empty-case result. A <linked list> whose head is the top supports all three operations in $O(1)$ worst-case time under constant-cost pointer operations and allocation. The <stack> interface itself does not fix these costs. https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/Stack.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms: Stack interface and implementation]{ref}

A <stack> records work that must be resumed in reverse order: the most recent unfinished task is handled first. <Depth-first search> uses this discipline to finish a newly entered branch before resuming the older branch that led to it. <Breadth-first search> uses a <FIFO queue> to resume the oldest pending <vertex> instead. Both are small interfaces whose removal rule changes the shape of a <graph search>.

= Call stack
{parent=Stack (data structure)}
{wiki}

A <call stack> stores active procedure calls and the information needed to resume each caller, such as its return location and local variables. A call adds a frame; returning removes that frame and resumes the previous call. When <depth-first search> uses <recursion>, a frame also records where its <adjacency list> scan paused. The active frames describe a <graph path> from the starting <vertex> to the current <vertex>; finished calls have left the <call stack>, although their <vertices> remain marked as discovered.

= Binary tree
{parent=Data structure}
{wiki}

A <binary tree>, in the <data structure> convention used here, is a <rooted tree> in which each <vertex> has zero, one or two <children in a rooted tree>, occupying distinct left and right positions. A single left child and a single right child give different <binary trees>. A <vertex> with no <children in a rooted tree> is called a leaf. An empty <binary tree> has no <root of a tree> and is also allowed as a storage case. https://opendatastructures.org/newhtml/ods/latex2/binarytrees.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, chapter 6: roots, left and right children]{ref}

For example, give the <root of a tree> label $4$, its left child label $2$ and its right child label $6$. Attach $1,3$ as the left and right <children in a rooted tree> of $2$, and $5,7$ as those of $6$. This <binary tree> has seven <vertices>, six <edges>, and <height of a binary tree> $2$: a longest downward <graph path> contains two <edges>. The first diagram below shows this shape alongside the <binary tree> consisting only of successive right children $1,2,\ldots,7$, whose <height of a binary tree> is $6$.

Left and right positions specify an ordered shape, not a numerical <total order> on stored labels. Swapping labels $1$ and $7$ in the first example still gives a <binary tree>, but destroys the <binary search tree> property. Nor must a <binary tree> be balanced: the right-child chain is just as valid. The same shape convention supports a <binary heap>, a <Huffman code>, or a <binary search tree>, each with its own additional rules. The useful idea is to describe two smaller <subtrees> at each <vertex>, making <structural recursion> natural.

= Subtree
{parent=Binary tree}

For a <vertex> $u$ in a <rooted tree>, its <subtree> contains $u$ and every <vertex> reachable by repeatedly following <children in a rooted tree>, together with the connecting <edges>; its <root of a tree> is $u$. In a <binary tree>, the left <subtree> is rooted at the left child, and is empty when that child is absent; the right <subtree> is defined similarly. A <binary search tree> constrains every key in these <subtrees>.

= Height of a binary tree
{parent=Binary tree}
{title2=$h$}

For a nonempty <binary tree>, the largest number of <edges> on a downward <graph path> from its <root of a tree> to a leaf. A single <vertex> has <height of a binary tree> $0$; a chain of $n\geq1$ <vertices> has <height of a binary tree> $n-1$. A convenient <recursion> convention assigns an empty <binary tree> height $-1$, giving $h=1+\max(h_L,h_R)$ for the left and right <subtrees>. A <binary search tree> lookup visits at most $h+1$ <vertices>.

= Inorder traversal
{parent=Binary tree}

An <inorder traversal> of a <binary tree> uses <recursion> to visit the left <subtree>, output the current <vertex>, and visit the right <subtree>; an empty <subtree> outputs nothing. This is a <depth-first traversal of a tree> with output between the two recursive calls. It produces sorted keys for a <binary search tree>, but arbitrary labels in a <binary tree> need not appear in <total order>.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Binary search tree
{parent=Binary tree}

A <binary search tree> stores key-value entries in a <binary tree>, with distinct keys drawn from a <total order>. At every <vertex>, all keys in its left <subtree> must be smaller than its own key, and all keys in its right <subtree> must be larger. Thus the entries represent a <partial function> from keys to values: a key has at most one associated value and may be absent. https://opendatastructures.org/newhtml/ods/latex2/binarytrees.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, section 6.2: the binary search tree property]{ref}

Lookup starts at the <root of a tree>. Compare the target with the current key: equality returns its value, a smaller target continues into the left <subtree>, and a larger target continues into the right <subtree>. Reaching an empty <subtree> reports absence. The ordering rule justifies discarding the other <subtree> at every step; this is the <algorithm correctness> argument for lookup. It applies to the entire <subtree>, even if that <subtree> contains many <vertices>. https://opendatastructures.org/newhtml/ods/latex2/binarytrees.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, section 6.2.1: exact-match searching]{ref}

In the seven-key example, search for $5$. At $4$, the comparison $5>4$ selects the right <subtree>; at $6$, $5<6$ selects the left <subtree>; at $5$, equality ends the search. The visited keys are $4\to6\to5$, three key comparisons. The right-child chain stores exactly the same keys and satisfies the same <binary search tree> rule, but lookup of $5$ visits $1\to2\to3\to4\to5$, five comparisons. Searching for absent key $8$ visits $4,6,7$ in the first <binary search tree> and all seven keys in the chain before reaching an empty right <subtree>.

\Image[binary-search-tree-search-path.png]
{title=Lookup of key $5$ in two <binary search trees> containing the same seven keys}
{description=Orange highlights visited keys and <edges>; green marks the match. The balanced shape has <height of a binary tree> $2$ and requires three visits, while the chain has <height of a binary tree> $6$ and requires five visits for this target.}

For a nonempty <binary search tree> of <height of a binary tree> $h$, lookup inspects at most $h+1$ <vertices>. Under constant-cost key comparisons and child-reference access, its <time complexity> is $O(h+1)$ in <Big O notation>, usually written $O(h)$ for $h\geq1$. With $n$ entries, $h\leq n-1$, giving $O(n)$ worst-case lookup. If the shape is kept balanced so that $h=O(\log n)$, lookup takes $O(\log n)$ for $n\geq2$. The empty and one-entry cases take constant work. The worst-case linear bound also appears in Sedgewick and Wayne's ordinary, unbalanced implementation. https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/BST.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms: BST implementation, complexity contract and get method]{ref}

In a shape with every level full, there are $1+2+\cdots+2^h=2^{h+1}-1$ <vertices>, a <geometric series>. This explains how a small <height of a binary tree> can accommodate many entries. The <binary search tree> ordering rule alone does not guarantee this shape or logarithmic <time complexity>. <Breadth-first search> also gains no logarithmic key-lookup guarantee merely from having a <binary tree>: in the displayed balanced example, its left-to-right level order is $4,2,6,1,3,5,7$, so it inspects six keys before finding $5$, and an absent target requires all seven. Directed lookup uses the <total order> to choose one branch.

The ordering requirement includes every ancestor on the <graph path> from the <root of a tree>. Consider the smaller <binary tree> with root key $4$, left child $2$, right child $6$, and right child $3$ under $2$. Key $3$ must satisfy both $2<3$, from its immediate <parent in a rooted tree>, and $3<4$, from its position in the root's left <subtree>. Replacing $3$ by $5$ preserves all immediate parent-child comparisons, including $2<5$, but violates $5<4$. Lookup for $5$ would incorrectly go from $4$ to $6$ and then to an empty left <subtree>, overlooking the stored key.

\Image[binary-search-tree-ordering-constraint.png]
{title=The <binary search tree> ordering rule constrains an entire <subtree>}
{description=The valid example has $2<3<4$. The invalid example passes immediate parent-child comparisons, but key $5$ lies in the left <subtree> of key $4$ and violates its inherited upper bound.}

An <inorder traversal> visits a left <subtree>, then its own <vertex>, then a right <subtree>. On the valid small example its output is $2,3,4,6$; on the invalid example it is $2,5,4,6$, exposing the descent from $5$ to $4$. Both seven-key <binary search trees> yield $1,2,3,4,5,6,7$. The theorem is that an <inorder traversal> of a finite <binary search tree> with distinct keys lists every key once in strictly increasing <total order>. For a short proof outline, use <mathematical induction> on the number of <vertices>: each smaller <subtree> produces its own sorted list, and the full ordering rule places every left key before the current key and every right key after it. Concatenating these three parts preserves strict order. Sedgewick and Wayne's `keys` method uses this left-current-right order. https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kevin-wayne/algs4/master/src/main/java/edu/princeton/cs/algs4/BST.java[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms: BST keys method and inherited-bound isBST check]{ref}

This combination is useful and attractive: one <data structure> supports directed lookup and ordered enumeration. An <inorder traversal> costs $O(n)$ <time complexity> for $n$ entries, since it visits every <vertex>; balance accelerates lookup but cannot eliminate the work of outputting all keys.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Dictionary intersection
{parent=Binary search tree}
{title2=$D_1\cap D_2$}

For partial maps $D_1,D_2$, retain exactly entries $(k,v)$ for which both maps define the same value $v$ at $k$. Merely sharing a key is insufficient. Traverse one <binary search tree>, look up each key in the other, and insert only equal-value matches into the result.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Complete binary tree
{parent=Binary tree}

A complete binary tree is a <binary tree> whose levels are full except possibly the deepest, which is filled from left to right. A <binary heap> uses this shape to maintain logarithmic height. Completeness differs from the two-children condition of a <full binary tree>.

= Full binary tree
{parent=Binary tree}

A full binary tree is a binary tree in which every nonleaf has exactly two children. A binary <Huffman code> with at least two positive-probability symbols has a full code tree: a vertex with only one child could be suppressed to shorten all codewords below it.

// Target: probability-and-statistics.bigb

= Linked list
{parent=Data structure}
{wiki}

A <data structure> representing a <sequence> through <linked list nodes> and links between them. In the singly linked representation used here, each <linked list node> holds an item and a `next` reference to its successor. `head` identifies the first <linked list node>; the final `next` is `null`, meaning no successor. An empty <linked list> has `head = null`. Sedgewick and Wayne use this representation to implement both a <stack> and a <FIFO queue>. https://algs4.cs.princeton.edu/13stacks/index.php[Sedgewick and Wayne, Algorithms, section 1.3: linked lists]{ref}

For $A\to B\to C$, place the <linked list nodes> at conceptual <computer memory> positions $40,12,76$, respectively. These deliberately separated labels describe a toy layout, not actual pointer values. The <sequence> order comes from the links, not increasing positions. To read index $2$, begin at `head` and follow two `next` references. For index $i$, this takes $i$ link-following steps and $O(1+i)$ <time complexity>, hence $O(n)$ in the worst case for $n$ <linked list nodes>, under the constant-cost operations assumed for the <array> example. https://opendatastructures.org/ods-java/3_Linked_Lists.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, chapter 3: links versus indexed access]{ref}

Suppose a reference to the existing <linked list node> $B$ is already known. To insert a fresh <linked list node> $X$ after it, first set `X.next = B.next`, preserving the old successor $C$, and then set `B.next = X`. The result is $A\to B\to X\to C$; none of the old items moves. Reversing these assignments would read the newly changed `B.next` and set `X.next = X`, losing the route to $C$ from `head`. This is a two-update <algorithm correctness> argument, rather than a claim that finding the insertion location is free.

\Image[linked-list-insertion-pointer-updates.png]
{title=Insert a <linked list node> by preserving the successor before redirecting the incoming link}

The two pointer updates take $O(1)$ time in <Big O notation>. The complete insertion has $O(1)$ <time complexity> if the predecessor is already known, allocating and initializing the fixed-size <linked list node> costs $O(1)$, and copying its item has constant cost. Finding $B$ from `head` can instead cost $O(n)$; a position described only by an index does not confer the constant-cost advantage. This makes a <linked list> useful when an <algorithm> already holds the needed <linked list node>, or repeatedly works at the front as a <stack> does.

Deletion similarly needs the incoming link: to remove $X$ after known $B$, set `B.next = X.next`. A reference to $X$ alone does not identify its predecessor in this singly linked representation. The head is a special case: after checking for a nonempty <linked list>, remove it with `head = head.next`. If a tail reference is also maintained, deleting the only <linked list node> must clear that reference too; a stored length must also be updated. Removing a <linked list node> from the <sequence> and reclaiming its storage are separate responsibilities. https://opendatastructures.org/ods-java/3_1_SLList_Singly_Linked_Li.html[Pat Morin, Open Data Structures, section 3.1: head removal and empty-list bookkeeping]{ref}

There are practical costs beyond <Big O notation>. Each singly linked <linked list node> needs a successor reference in addition to its item; separate allocation may add further overhead, while an <array> can reserve unused capacity. Consecutive <array> slots can make a scan reuse nearby contents already brought into a <memory cache>; separated <linked list nodes> can make link following wait for successive <computer memory> fetches. These are layout-dependent effects, not a guarantee that every <array> operation beats every <linked list> operation on every machine. An <array> of references can still lead to scattered objects, and <linked list nodes> can be allocated close together. https://people.freebsd.org/~lstewart/articles/cpumemory.pdf[Ulrich Drepper, What Every Programmer Should Know About Memory, sections 3.3 and 6.2: access patterns and cache use]{ref}

= Linked list node
{parent=Linked list}

One record holding an item and the references that connect it into a <linked list>. In the singly linked examples, its `next` identifies the successor or is `null`. A <linked list node>'s position in the <sequence> can change when links change, without moving the <linked list node> in <computer memory>.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Mutable list
{parent=Linked list}

A <linked list> whose links are held in <ML reference type> cells and may be changed. Deleting a node updates the link that reaches it, including the root link when the head is removed. Aliases of links observe these mutations; no fresh list spine is needed for destructive filtering.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Lazy list
{parent=Linked list}

A <linked list> whose future structure is obtained by calling a delayed computation. In <Standard ML>, `unit -> node` delays a computation until demand. Filtering may need to force arbitrarily many rejected elements to produce its next retained element; filtering an infinite list need not produce a result. Delay alone does not imply <memoization>.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Programming language
{parent=Computer science}
{wiki}

A <programming language> specifies how programs are written and what their execution means. Its <type system> describes which operations may be combined; an <abstract syntax tree> records the structure on which these rules act. The examples under <type system> use a small teaching language with <integers>, Boolean values and <function types>.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Bit mask
{parent=Programming language}

A word selecting specified binary positions under a bitwise operation. To extract a stored bit at position $b$, logically shift by $b$ and combine with the mask `1`. Grouping thirty-two cells into one <Java> integer permits a compact representation of a <Conway Game of Life> board.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Bit shift
{parent=Programming language}

A fixed-width left shift discards high bits and inserts low zeros. A logical right shift inserts high zeros; an arithmetic right shift propagates the sign bit. In <Java>, `<<`, `>>>` and `>>` distinguish these operations. Left shift by $k$ is multiplication by $2^k$ modulo the word modulus.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Two's complement
{parent=Programming language}
{title2=$\mathbb Z/2^w\mathbb Z$}

For a fixed word width $w$, signed integers have the same bit patterns as residues modulo $2^w$. The leading bit has negative positional weight in the signed interpretation. Reading the same bits as unsigned gives $2^w+x$ for a negative signed integer $x$.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Hexadecimal
{parent=Programming language}

Base-sixteen representation, with digit values zero through fifteen conventionally written `0` through `9` and `a` through `f`. A group of four binary digits selects one hexadecimal digit.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Abstract syntax tree
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}
{title2=AST}

= AST
{c}
{synonym}

An <abstract syntax tree> is a <rooted tree> recording the meaningful syntactic pieces of a program and how they fit together. A <program identifier> such as `x`, a <literal value> such as `2`, and a node representing <addition> have different roles. Their arrangement answers structural questions: which <program expression> supplies an operand, which name is a <variable assignment> target, and which construct encloses another. This makes an <abstract syntax tree> useful to a <compiler>, an <interpreter>, and the <type system> that checks a program.

The word "abstract" describes what is omitted. <Lexical analysis> first recognizes <lexical tokens>, such as `x`, `=`, `2`, `+`, `3`, `*`, `4`. A concrete <parse tree> can retain <grammar derivation> structure, including punctuation and intermediate <context-free grammar> categories such as expression, term and factor. An <abstract syntax tree> usually keeps the operation and its operands without all those grammar scaffolds. Parentheses can determine its shape without surviving as separate nodes. A <parser> may construct the <abstract syntax tree> directly; it need not first allocate a concrete <parse tree>.

An <abstract syntax tree> records <programming language syntax>, but does not by itself supply all <programming language semantics>. In `a+b`, the names do not reveal their current values, and `+` might perform <addition> of <integers> or concatenate strings. The <programming language> rules, name bindings, and operand types determine the meaning. In <Python>, successfully calling `ast.parse` also does not guarantee that later <compilation> will accept the result: parsing `return 42` succeeds, but compiling that statement outside a function fails. <Python 3.14 documentation/ast — Abstract syntax trees, Python 3.14 documentation>[ast.parse: parsing performs no scoping checks; the return 42 example]{ref}

An <abstract syntax tree> is not <machine code>. Its nodes describe language constructs. A <compiler> can check and transform this structure before translating it into executable instructions; an <interpreter> can evaluate an appropriate tree directly. The little evaluator below demonstrates this distinction without pretending to implement an entire <programming language>.

= Abstract syntax tree precedence example
{parent=Abstract syntax tree}

Consider <Python> programs `x=2+3*4` and `x=(2+3)*4`. <Operator precedence> places <multiplication> inside the right operand of <addition> in the first <abstract syntax tree>. The parentheses instead place <addition> inside the left operand of <multiplication> in the second. Their right-hand sides evaluate to $2+(3\cdot4)=14$ and $(2+3)\cdot4=20$. No rule that simply reads all operators left to right would recover both structures. https://docs.python.org/3.14/reference/expressions.html#operator-precedence[Python language reference, section 6.17, multiplicative operators bind more tightly than additive operators]{ref}

\Image[abstract-syntax-tree-precedence.png]
{id=image-abstract-syntax-tree-precedence}
{title=Two <abstract syntax trees> for <variable assignment>: <operator precedence> gives `x=2+3*4` the value $14$, while parentheses give `x=(2+3)*4` the value $20$}

These are schematic views of <Python> trees: `Module` is omitted, and the `Add`/`Mult` and `Store` markers are written inside their owning nodes. In the actual schema, `BinOp` has `left`, `op` and `right` fields; `Assign` has a list of `targets` and a `value`. `Name(id='x', ctx=Store())` marks the name as a <variable assignment> target. Reading `x` in a <program expression> instead uses `Load`. A <variable assignment> is a statement, not an <equation> asserting that two values are equal. <Abstract syntax schema for CPython 3.14>[CPython 3.14, Parser/Python.asdl: Module, Assign, BinOp, Constant, Name and expr_context constructors]{ref}

A restricted recursive evaluator can follow those fields. It accepts only <integer> <literal values> and binary <addition>, <subtraction> and <multiplication>; the surrounding <variable assignment> remains a separate action. Using `type(value) is int` excludes Boolean constants, even though <Python> treats `bool` as a subclass of `int`.

```
import ast

OPS = {ast.Add: lambda a, b: a + b,
       ast.Sub: lambda a, b: a - b,
       ast.Mult: lambda a, b: a * b}

def evaluate(node):
    if isinstance(node, ast.Constant) and type(node.value) is int:
        return node.value
    if isinstance(node, ast.BinOp) and type(node.op) in OPS:
        return OPS[type(node.op)](evaluate(node.left), evaluate(node.right))
    raise ValueError("outside the integer-expression language")

for source in ("x=2+3*4", "x=(2+3)*4"):
    tree = ast.parse(source, optimize=0)
    statement = tree.body[0]
    print(statement.targets[0].id, evaluate(statement.value))
# x 14
# x 20
```

The <children in a rooted tree> have ordered roles: `left` and `right` cannot be interchanged. For <subtraction>, `8-3` gives $5$ while `3-8` gives $-5$. <Operator associativity> also matters: `8-3-1` parses as `(8-3)-1`, giving $4$, whereas `8-(3-1)` gives $6$. Here the syntax groups operators to the left; this does not assert the mathematical <associativity> of <subtraction>. <Operator precedence> determines grouping, rather than a general permission to reorder execution. For example, <Python> evaluates the calls in `f()+g()*h()` in the order `f`, `g`, `h`, although the <multiplication> result must be ready before the outer <addition>. https://docs.python.org/3.14/reference/expressions.html#evaluation-order[Python language reference, sections 6.16 and 6.17: left-to-right evaluation and left grouping of additive operators]{ref}

= Abstract syntax tree traversal
{parent=Abstract syntax tree}

An <abstract syntax tree> traversal systematically visits its nodes. A <depth-first traversal of a tree> that processes each child before its parent is called postorder. For the <program expression> `2+3*4`, the schematic postorder is `2, 3, 4, Mult, Add`. This order lets an evaluator obtain each operand value before applying its operation. A traversal of the entire <Python> schema additionally visits marker nodes such as `Add` and `Store`; this shorter list concerns only the expression nodes drawn in the figures. Visiting an <abstract syntax tree> is a structural operation, and is not a general model of execution: a conditional may select only one branch, while a structural visitor examines both.

= Abstract syntax tree transformation
{parent=Abstract syntax tree}

An <abstract syntax tree> transformation replaces or rearranges nodes. It can simplify a <program expression>, insert instrumentation, or translate a language construct into simpler constructs. When intended to preserve <programming language semantics>, it must preserve the relevant observable behavior, including values, <side effects>, and failures. A changed tree can be structurally valid while changing the program's meaning.

= Constant folding
{parent=Abstract syntax tree transformation}
{wiki}

<Constant folding> replaces a <program expression> whose value is known during translation by a <literal value>. For `x=2+3*4`, first replace the <multiplication> subtree by `Constant(value=12)`, then replace the <addition> subtree by `Constant(value=14)`. The <variable assignment> and its `Store` target survive: the result still assigns to `x`. This illustrates why a bottom-up <abstract syntax tree traversal> is convenient: replacing one child can make its parent eligible for the next replacement.

\Image[abstract-syntax-tree-constant-folding.png]
{id=image-abstract-syntax-tree-constant-folding}
{title=<Constant folding> and postorder <abstract syntax tree traversal> for `2+3*4`: replace $3\cdot4$ by $12$, then $2+12$ by $14$}

Here is a deliberately small <Python> transformer. Calling `generic_visit` first transforms the children; returning a node replaces the visited node. The location helpers retain or supply the metadata expected for <compilation>. <Python 3.14 documentation/ast — Abstract syntax trees, Python 3.14 documentation>[NodeTransformer, generic_visit, copy_location and fix_missing_locations]{ref}

```
class FoldIntegers(ast.NodeTransformer):
    def visit_BinOp(self, node):
        self.generic_visit(node)
        if (isinstance(node.left, ast.Constant)
                and isinstance(node.right, ast.Constant)
                and type(node.left.value) is int
                and type(node.right.value) is int
                and type(node.op) in OPS):
            value = OPS[type(node.op)](node.left.value, node.right.value)
            return ast.copy_location(ast.Constant(value=value), node)
        return node

tree = ast.parse("x=2+3*4", optimize=0)
folded = ast.fix_missing_locations(FoldIntegers().visit(tree))
namespace = {}
exec(compile(folded, "<folded example>", "exec"), namespace)
assert namespace["x"] == 14
assert ast.unparse(folded) == "x = 14"
```

This transformer folds literal <integer> operands only. It leaves `a+3*4` as `a+12`, without inventing a value for the <program identifier> `a`. It leaves calls intact: replacing `0*f()` by `0` could discard a <side effect> or a failure. Nor does it reassociate <floating-point arithmetic>, where changing parentheses can change rounding. For the supported <integer arithmetic>, a short <structural induction> explains correctness: a retained <literal value> has the same value; at each folded binary node, the already-correct child values are combined by exactly the original operation in the original left/right order. This claim concerns the supported fragment's values, not unrestricted equivalence of arbitrary <Python> programs.

= Abstract syntax tree source location
{parent=Abstract syntax tree}

An <abstract syntax tree> can retain source locations even while omitting punctuation nodes. <Python> records one-based line numbers and zero-based UTF-8 byte offsets, with the end offset exclusive. In `x=2+3*4`, the <multiplication> node spans offsets $4$ through $7$, and `ast.get_source_segment` returns `3*4`. A copied location on a folded `12` describes the original span, not text newly present in the source. Such metadata supports error messages and editing tools without making an <abstract syntax tree> a lossless record of formatting. <Python 3.14 documentation/ast — Abstract syntax trees, Python 3.14 documentation>[Node classes: lineno, col_offset, end_lineno and end_col_offset; get_source_segment and copy_location]{ref}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Programming language syntax
{parent=Programming language}

The rules for forming programs in a <programming language>. A <parser> checks and structures text according to these rules; an <abstract syntax tree> records the resulting constructs.

= Lexical analysis
{parent=Programming language syntax}
{wiki}

Recognition of <lexical tokens> in program text before or during <parsing>.

= Lexical token
{parent=Lexical analysis}

A categorized piece of program text, such as a <program identifier>, a numeral denoting a <literal value>, or an operator symbol. Its spelling and category help a <parser> recognize the larger construct.

= Parser
{parent=Programming language syntax}

A parser recognizes how text fits a <programming language syntax> and can construct a <parse tree> or <abstract syntax tree>.

= Parsing
{parent=Parser}

The recognition process performed by a <parser>, producing a structural description of text according to a <programming language syntax>.

= Program identifier
{parent=Programming language syntax}

A name used by a program, such as `x` in a <variable assignment>. An <abstract syntax tree> can record the name without determining which value it denotes.

= Literal value
{parent=Programming language syntax}

A value written directly in program text, such as an <integer> numeral or a quoted string, rather than obtained through a <program identifier>. Distinct spellings can denote the same value.

= Program expression
{parent=Programming language syntax}

A program construct that is evaluated to obtain a value, potentially with <side effects> or failure. An arithmetic <mathematical expression> is one example; its <abstract syntax tree> records how operands and operations nest.

= Operator precedence
{parent=Program expression}

The syntactic priority that resolves grouping when different operators occur together without parentheses. It determines the <abstract syntax tree> shape before evaluation.

= Operator associativity
{parent=Operator precedence}

The syntactic grouping convention for successive operators of the same precedence. Left grouping of <subtraction> makes `a-b-c` mean `(a-b)-c`; it does not make <subtraction> an <associative> operation.

= Variable assignment
{parent=Programming language syntax}

A program action that associates a value with a target name. For <Python> `x=2+3*4`, evaluate the right-hand <program expression> and bind `x` to its result. https://docs.python.org/3.14/reference/simple_stmts.html#assignment-statements[Python language reference, section 7.2: assignment to an identifier and evaluation of the right-hand side]{ref}

= Programming language semantics
{parent=Programming language}

The meaning of constructs in a <programming language>, including their permitted values and execution behavior. <Programming language syntax> organizes constructs; a <type system> and execution rules give further constraints and meaning.

= Compiler
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

A compiler translates a program into another representation, often executable instructions. <Compilation> can use an <abstract syntax tree> for checking and transformations such as <constant folding> before producing <machine code>.

= Compilation
{parent=Compiler}

The translation process performed by a <compiler>, which may include checking and transforming an <abstract syntax tree>.

= Interpreter
{disambiguate=computing}
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

= Interpreter
{synonym}

An implementation that executes a program's operations. An <interpreter> can work from an <abstract syntax tree> or another representation rather than requiring the whole program to be translated into <machine code> in advance.

= Machine code
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

Encoded instructions executed by a <central processing unit>. An <abstract syntax tree> instead describes <programming language> constructs and their nesting.

= Python
{c}
{disambiguate=programming language}
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

= Python
{c}
{synonym}

A <programming language> whose standard-library `ast` module provides access to its <abstract syntax trees>. The examples here use its version 3.14 schema and literal <integer arithmetic>.

= Object-oriented programming
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Encapsulation in object-oriented programming
{parent=Object-oriented programming}

In <object-oriented programming>, encapsulation packages an object's state with the operations that act on it and can restrict direct access to its implementation. It is the object-oriented use of <encapsulation (computer programming)>.

= Class
{disambiguate=programming}
{parent=Object-oriented programming}
{wiki}

= Class in object-oriented programming
{synonym}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Java
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}
{c}
{disambiguate=programming language}

= Java
{c}
{synonym}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Java interface
{parent=Java (programming language)}
{c}

An interface specifies a type contract that independent classes can implement. Code accepting the interface can operate on multiple implementations, avoiding dependence on one concrete class. A class may implement several interfaces despite having only one direct class superclass.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Private field in Java
{parent=Java (programming language)}

= Private fields in Java
{synonym}

A field declared `private` is hidden from ordinary external source-level access. Library methods can enforce invariants and change the representation without exposing storage to clients. Returning a mutable internal object can still undermine this <encapsulation in object-oriented programming>.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Final class in Java
{parent=Java (programming language)}

A class declared `final` cannot be subclassed. This can protect an implementation contract against overriding in an untrusted subclass. It does not by itself make the class immutable, and it rules out subclass-based customization.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Protected method in Java
{parent=Java (programming language)}

A `protected` method is accessible to code in its package and, subject to the cross-package receiver restriction, to subclasses elsewhere. It can expose a subclass customization hook without being a public client operation. Protected access is broader than subclass-only access.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Java array
{parent=Java (programming language)}
{c}

Java arrays are fixed-length objects. A two-dimensional array is an array of row-array references, so rows can have different lengths and must be allocated separately if not allocated by the initial expression.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Generics in Java
{parent=Java (programming language)}
{wiki}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Generic method in Java
{parent=Generics in Java}

A method declaring its own type parameters, for example `<T> List<T> singletonList(T value)`. The type parameter connects argument and result types and permits compile-time checking without casts at ordinary call sites. A method mentioning only a generic class's type parameter need not itself be a generic method.

// Destination: computer-science.bigb

= Generic type invariance
{parent=Generics in Java}

Even when `S` is a subtype of `T`, neither `G<S>` nor `G<T>` is generally a subtype of the other. This prevents unsafe writes into mutable generic containers. Wildcards express restricted views.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Type erasure
{parent=Generics in Java}
{wiki}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Exception handling
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= ML exception type
{parent=Exception handling}
{c}
{title2=`exn`}

In <Standard ML>, `exn` is an extensible datatype of exception packets. Fresh declarations introduce constructors, optionally with payloads; rebinding can alias an existing constructor; `raise` propagates a packet and `handle` performs dynamic pattern-based recovery.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Type system
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

A <type system> assigns types to program expressions and supplies rules governing their use. A type constrains permitted operations: in our teaching <programming language>, `int` admits <integer> addition, `bool` supplies `true` and `false` for conditions, and a <function type> specifies an argument type and a result type. Storage as <bits> alone does not explain these distinctions; the <type system> determines whether an expression can serve as an argument, a condition or an operand.

A <typing judgment> $\Gamma\vdash e:\tau$ says that expression $e$ has type $\tau$ under the assumptions in the <typing context> $\Gamma$. For example,
$$
\Gamma=\{x:\mathrm{int},y:\mathrm{int}\},\qquad \Gamma\vdash x+y:\mathrm{int}.
$$
The assumptions give each operand type `int`; the addition rule then gives the sum type `int`. Changing $y$ to type `bool` prevents this derivation. Our teaching <programming language> has distinct `int` and `bool` types and no implicit conversions between them.

The conditional rule requires a Boolean condition and the same type for both branches:
$$
\frac{\Gamma\vdash c:\mathrm{bool}\qquad\Gamma\vdash e_1:\tau\qquad\Gamma\vdash e_2:\tau}{\Gamma\vdash\mathrm{if}\ c\ \mathrm{then}\ e_1\ \mathrm{else}\ e_2:\tau}.
$$
Thus `if true then 1 else 2` has type `int`, while `if true then 1 else false` is rejected: its branches would require $\tau=\mathrm{int}$ and $\tau=\mathrm{bool}$ simultaneously. Even a branch that will not execute is checked by this rule. This is a rule of this particular <type system>; a different <programming language> can choose different rules. https://softwarefoundations.cis.upenn.edu/plf-current/Types.html[Software Foundations, Programming Language Foundations, Type Systems: typing relation]{ref}

The useful feature is compositional reasoning: after checking the parts, a rule explains how to check the whole. The <function type> diagram shows this for a call. <Type inference> can discover the required types automatically, and <type variables> let one express relationships that recur across many concrete types. The result is both a practical early error detector and a small, readable derivation explaining why an expression fits its intended use.

The guarantee depends on the rules and execution model. <Type safety> can exclude specified failures such as adding a Boolean or calling an <integer> as a function. It does not by itself establish every desired runtime property or guarantee that execution terminates. A <type system> that permits unrestricted recursion can accept a computation that runs forever; <exception handling> also needs its own account of permitted outcomes.

= Simple type
{parent=Type system}
{title2=$\tau$}

In the examples here, a <simple type> is a finite expression built from the base types `int` and `bool` using <function types>:
$$
\tau::=\mathrm{int}\mid\mathrm{bool}\mid(\tau\to\tau).
$$
For example, $\mathrm{int}\to\mathrm{bool}$ describes a function accepting an <integer> and producing a Boolean value. During <type inference>, the grammar is extended with <type variables> such as $\alpha$ for types not yet determined. A <type scheme> additionally binds such <type variables>; it is distinct from the individual <simple type> used at one call.

= Typing judgment
{parent=Type system}
{title2=$\Gamma\vdash e:\tau$}

A <typing judgment> asserts that expression $e$ has type $\tau$ under a <typing context> $\Gamma$. A rule displays prerequisite <typing judgments> above a line and their conclusion below it. Repeatedly applying rules gives a derivation; the addition example under <type system> uses two assumptions and one addition rule.

= Typing context
{parent=Typing judgment}
{title2=$\Gamma$}

A <typing context> records the types assumed for available program names. In $\Gamma=\{x:\mathrm{int},y:\mathrm{int}\}$, looking up either name yields `int`. Checking a <lambda abstraction> extends the <typing context> with its argument's type for the body. In the <Hindley-Milner type system>, a <typing context> can also hold <type schemes> for names introduced by <let polymorphism>.

= Type safety
{parent=Type system}
{wiki}

For a specified <programming language> and execution model, <type safety> commonly follows from two properties: a closed expression satisfying a <typing judgment> is either a value or can take an execution step, and taking a step preserves its type. Proofs proceed by <structural induction> on derivations, using <capture-avoiding substitution> for function calls. Repeated preservation keeps the remaining expression typed; the first property then rules out getting stuck at an operation outside the model's permitted cases. This argument does not imply termination or correctness of an arbitrary specification. https://softwarefoundations.cis.upenn.edu/plf-current/Types.html[Software Foundations, Programming Language Foundations, Type Systems: progress, preservation and type soundness]{ref}

= Type inference
{parent=Type system}
{wiki}

<Type inference> discovers types consistent with a program's structure and <typing context>. In our examples, it introduces fresh <type variables>, generates <equations> from <typing judgments>, and solves them by <type unification>. For $f\,3$, if $f$ initially has unknown type $\alpha$ and the result has fresh type $\beta$, <lambda application> requires $\alpha=\mathrm{int}\to\beta$.

= Type unification
{parent=Type inference}

<Type unification> finds a <type substitution> making specified type expressions equal, or reports incompatible constraints. In our <simple type> grammar, matching two <function types> reduces to matching their argument types and their result types. Distinct base types `int` and `bool` fail to match; solving a <type variable> against a nontrivial type expression uses the <occurs check>.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Type variable
{parent=Type system}
{wiki}
{title2=$\alpha$}

A <type variable> is a placeholder for a type. In a <function type> $\alpha\to\alpha$, the repeated <type variable> requires the argument and result types to agree; $\alpha\to\beta$ does not impose that agreement. A <type substitution> replacing $\alpha$ by `int` changes $\alpha\to\alpha$ to $\mathrm{int}\to\mathrm{int}$ everywhere within that instance.

For the <identity function> written as the <lambda abstraction> $\lambda x.x$, assume $x:\alpha$ in the body. Looking up $x$ gives result type $\alpha$, so the abstraction rule gives $\lambda x.x:\alpha\to\alpha$. In the pure <Hindley-Milner type system>, `let identity = λx.x in ...` gives `identity` the <type scheme> $\forall\alpha.\alpha\to\alpha$ by <let polymorphism>. Each use receives fresh <type variables>: `identity 3` chooses `int` for its instance, and `identity true` chooses `bool` for a separate instance. These choices do not change the original <type scheme>.

\Image[polymorphic-identity-instantiation.png]
{title=Independent type instantiations of a let-bound identity function}
{description=The <type scheme> stays general while two uses receive different <type substitutions>. Each use preserves the equality of its own argument and result types.}

For example, `let identity = λx.x in let a = identity 3 in identity true` is valid and has type `bool`. By contrast, `(λf. let a = f 3 in f true) (λx.x)` is rejected by the <Hindley-Milner type system>: the <lambda abstraction> binds `f` to one monomorphic <function type>. The first call requires its argument type to be `int`, and the second requires that same type to be `bool`. Naming the first result with `let a` does not generalize `f`. The distinction makes reusable code possible while keeping the assumptions about a supplied argument consistent.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Type substitution
{parent=Type variable}
{title2=$S$}

A <type substitution> replaces <type variables> by type expressions consistently. For example, $S=\{\alpha\mapsto\mathrm{int}\}$ gives $S(\alpha\to\alpha)=\mathrm{int}\to\mathrm{int}$. Applying a <type substitution> to a <type scheme> must respect its bound <type variables>; separate instantiation first replaces those bound placeholders by fresh ones.

= Type scheme
{parent=Type variable}
{title2=$\forall\alpha.\tau$}

A <type scheme> binds <type variables> that may be instantiated independently at different uses. The <identity function> has <type scheme> $\forall\alpha.\alpha\to\alpha$: choose one type per use, then use that same choice on both sides of its <function type>. A free <type variable> shared with the <typing context> is not automatically such an independently selectable parameter.

= Occurs check
{parent=Type variable}

The <occurs check> tests whether a <type variable> occurs inside the expression proposed as its replacement during <type unification>. After discarding a trivial <equation> such as $\alpha=\alpha$, solving $\alpha=\tau$ by a <type substitution> requires that $\alpha$ not occur in $\tau$. Thus $\alpha=\mathrm{int}\to\beta$ is permitted when $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are distinct, but $\alpha=\alpha\to\beta$ is rejected in our finite <simple type> grammar.

There is a short reason for the rejection. If a finite replacement $T$ for $\alpha$ existed, with replacement $B$ for $\beta$, the <equation> would become $T=T\to B$. The right side's <abstract syntax tree> has one new arrow node plus the nodes of $T$ and $B$, so it has strictly more nodes than $T$. Equal finite type expressions cannot have different sizes. <Recursive types> are a different design: they explicitly describe self-reference and specify how it interacts with <typing judgments>.

= Recursive type
{parent=Type system}
{title2=$\mu\alpha.\tau$}

A <recursive type> explicitly names a type in terms of itself. Notation $\mu\alpha.\tau$ binds $\alpha$ within $\tau$. A <programming language> must specify whether unfolding gives type equality or requires explicit operations; this is outside the finite <simple type> grammar used here. See Benjamin C. Pierce's treatment of <recursive types> in chapters 20–21. https://www.cis.upenn.edu/~bcpierce/tapl/contents.pdf[Pierce, Types and Programming Languages (2002), author-hosted contents, chapters 20–21]{ref}

= Function type
{parent=Type system}
{wiki}
{title2=$\tau_1\to\tau_2$}

A <function type> $\tau_1\to\tau_2$ classifies functions that accept arguments of type $\tau_1$ and produce results of type $\tau_2$ if they return. For example, $\lambda x.x+1$ has <function type> $\mathrm{int}\to\mathrm{int}$ in our teaching <programming language>, while $\lambda b.\mathrm{if}\ b\ \mathrm{then}\ 1\ \mathrm{else}\ 2$ has <function type> $\mathrm{bool}\to\mathrm{int}$. A <function type> specifies how a function may be used; it does not guarantee that a call returns.

\Image[function-typechecking-tree.png]
{title=Checking an integer function call from its parts}
{description=An addition rule checks the body of a <lambda abstraction>, the abstraction rule gives its <function type>, and <lambda application> checks the argument and obtains the result type. Arrows run from prerequisite <typing judgments> to their conclusions.}

The two central <typing judgment> rules are
$$
\frac{\Gamma,x:\tau_1\vdash e:\tau_2}{\Gamma\vdash\lambda x.e:\tau_1\to\tau_2},\qquad
\frac{\Gamma\vdash f:\tau_1\to\tau_2\qquad\Gamma\vdash a:\tau_1}{\Gamma\vdash f\,a:\tau_2}.
$$
The <lambda abstraction> rule checks the body under an extended <typing context>; the <lambda application> rule checks that the argument has exactly the required type. These rules describe our <simple type> examples. https://softwarefoundations.cis.upenn.edu/plf-current/Stlc.html[Software Foundations, Programming Language Foundations, The Simply Typed Lambda-Calculus: typing rules]{ref}

For $(\lambda x.x+1)\,3$, assume $x:\mathrm{int}$; both $x$ and $1$ have type `int`, so $x+1$ has type `int`. The abstraction therefore has <function type> $\mathrm{int}\to\mathrm{int}$. Since $3$ has type `int`, the call has type `int` and evaluates to $4$. Replacing $3$ by `true` fails the argument requirement. This local derivation explains each step without running the program.

Arrows associate to the right: $\tau_1\to\tau_2\to\tau_3$ means $\tau_1\to(\tau_2\to\tau_3)$, a function returning another function. For instance, $\lambda x.\lambda y.x+y$ has <function type> $\mathrm{int}\to(\mathrm{int}\to\mathrm{int})$. Supplying one <integer> yields a function awaiting the second. Functions can also be arguments: $(\mathrm{int}\to\mathrm{int})\to\mathrm{int}$ accepts an <integer>-to-<integer> function and has an <integer> result type.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Parametric polymorphism
{parent=Type system}
{wiki}

A function or data structure is parameterized by types and can operate uniformly over their instantiations.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Hindley-Milner type system
{parent=Type system}
{c}
{wiki=Hindley–Milner_type_system}

The pure <Hindley-Milner type system> combines <function types>, <type inference> and <let polymorphism>. It solves constraints by <type unification> and generalizes eligible <type variables> at `let` bindings. Robin Milner's 1978 paper presents <Algorithm W> and proves its soundness for the paper's language; it also acknowledges Roger Hindley's earlier work on finding general types. https://www.pure.ed.ac.uk/ws/files/15143545/1_s2.0_0022000078900144_main.pdf[Milner, A Theory of Type Polymorphism in Programming (1978), introduction and sections 3.5, 4.1–4.2]{ref}

= Algorithm W
{parent=Hindley-Milner type system}
{c}

<Algorithm W> performs <type inference> by traversing the program's <abstract syntax tree>, introducing fresh <type variables>, combining <type substitutions> obtained by <type unification>, and generalizing eligible `let` bindings. It distinguishes fresh instances of a <type scheme> from the shared monomorphic types of parameters to a <lambda abstraction>.

= Let polymorphism
{parent=Hindley-Milner type system}

<Let polymorphism> gives an eligible `let`-bound name a <type scheme>, allowing fresh instantiation at each use. In the pure <Hindley-Milner type system>, generalize the inferred type's <type variables> that are free in that type and not free in the current <typing context>. The <identity function> example under <type variable> meets this condition. A <lambda abstraction>'s parameter receives one monomorphic type shared by its uses. Languages with mutation, such as <Standard ML> with <ML reference types>, need additional restrictions on generalization.

= Imperative programming
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

= Side effect
{disambiguate=computer science}
{parent=Imperative programming}
{wiki}

= Side effect
{synonym}

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Functional programming
{parent=Programming language}
{wiki}

= Pure function
{parent=Functional programming}
{wiki}

A procedure whose result depends only on its explicit argument values and which has no observable <side effects>. For terminating calls with reusable results, this makes the value condition of the <memoization cache contract> particularly simple.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Structural recursion
{parent=Functional programming}
{wiki}

Recursion following the constructors of a finite inductive datatype. Correctness is naturally proved by <structural induction>.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Standard ML
{parent=Programming language}
{c}
{wiki}

= SML
{c}
{synonym}

A strict functional <programming language> with <parametric polymorphism>, algebraic datatypes, <exception handling> and mutable references.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= ML reference type
{parent=Standard ML}
{c}
{title2=`'a ref`}

A mutable cell holding a value of type `'a`. `ref` allocates a cell, `!` reads it and `:=` updates it. Aliases share the same cell; the value restriction prevents unsafe polymorphic mutable storage.

// Target: computer-science.bigb

= Linial-Mansour-Nisan theorem
{parent=Computer science}
{c}

The Linial-Mansour-Nisan theorem bounds the high-degree Fourier weight of a Boolean function computed by a small bounded-depth circuit.

= Image processing
{parent=Computer science}

<Image processing> transforms recorded <image signal> data to remove noise, recover detail or extract structure. <Variational image processing> describes desired reconstructions through energies, while <diffusion image processing> describes filtering through evolution equations.

= Quantization
{disambiguate=signal processing}
{parent=Image processing}
{wiki}
{title2=$Q(x)$}

<Quantization (signal processing)> maps numerical values to a specified discrete collection of representable values. In <lossy block averaging>, $Q(m)=\lfloor m+1/2\rfloor$ maps block <arithmetic means> in $[0,255]$ to eight-<bit> <integers>. Thus $Q(40.25)=40$ and $Q(40.5)=41$. Different means can share a stored value, making this step irreversible.

= Nearest-neighbor interpolation
{parent=Image processing}
{wiki}

<Nearest-neighbor interpolation> assigns each requested location the value of its nearest available sample, with a specified convention for ties. In <lossy block averaging>, samples are placed at block centres and each is copied into its four original <pixel> positions. This enlarges the <raster image> without recovering discarded detail.

= Image segmentation
{parent=Image processing}
{wiki}

<Image segmentation> divides an <image signal> domain into regions according to a chosen criterion, such as approximately constant or smoothly varying intensity. The <Mumford–Shah segmentation model> balances fidelity, within-region smoothness and <image edge> length. An intensity segmentation need not coincide with semantic object identities.

= Image signal
{parent=Image processing}
{title2=$u:\Omega\to\mathbb R$}

A grey-value <image signal> is a scalar function on an <image signal> domain or an array of pixel values. Continuous <image processing> models use spaces such as $L^2$, <Sobolev spaces> or the <BV space>, while discrete models use finite-dimensional arrays. The chosen representation distinguishes smooth transitions, noise and <image edges>.

= Image noise
{parent=Image signal}
{title2=$g=u+\eta$}

Image noise is unwanted variation in an observed <image signal>. In an additive model $g=u+\eta$, Gaussian noise motivates a squared data-fidelity penalty, while other statistics require different penalties. <Image smoothing> suppresses rapid fluctuations, but a filter must distinguish noise from genuine <image edges>.

= Structure tensor
{parent=Image processing}
{title2=$J_\rho=G_\rho*(\nabla u_\sigma\nabla u_\sigma^T)$}

For a smoothed <image signal> $u_\sigma$, average the <gradient> outer product as $J_\rho=G_\rho*(\nabla u_\sigma\nabla u_\sigma^T)$. The dominant <eigenvector> estimates the normal to a coherent <image edge>, and <eigenvalue> contrast <measures> local directional structure. Two distinct scales separate differentiation from orientation averaging.

= Image edge
{parent=Image processing}

An <image edge> is a rapid grey-value transition, modeled as a large <gradient> or a jump discontinuity. It differs from an <edge of a graph>. Variational models can penalize <image edge> length, while <diffusion image processing> estimates <image edge> directions to limit <image smoothing> across them.

= Image edge enhancement
{parent=Image edge}

<image edge> enhancement increases the apparent sharpness or contrast of an <image edge>. <Unsharp masking> gives bounded linear high-frequency amplification; backward normal diffusion in the <Perona-Malik equation> gives formal nonlinear sharpening with <ill-posedness> risks. Positive diffusion tensors chiefly preserve or connect structure rather than perform unbounded backward diffusion.

= Shock filter for image enhancement
{parent=Image edge enhancement}
{title2=$u_t=-\operatorname{sign}(\Delta u)|\nabla u|$}

The formal shock-filter equation $u_t=-\operatorname{sign}(\Delta u)|\nabla u|$ steepens transitions around inflection boundaries. It is a Hamilton–Jacobi-type transport mechanism rather than positive diffusion. Combining it with controlled forward <image smoothing> limits noise amplification.

= Unsharp masking
{parent=Image edge enhancement}
{title2=$g+\lambda(g-G_t*g)$}

Subtract a smoothed <image signal> and add a scaled residual: $u=g+\lambda(g-G_t*g)$. The Fourier multiplier $1+\lambda(1-e^{-t|\xi|^2})$ is bounded by $1+\lambda$. This enhances apparent contrast but also amplifies noise; it is not a stable exact inverse of heat <image smoothing>.

= Diffusion image processing
{parent=Image processing}

Diffusion filtering evolves an observed <image signal> using a <diffusion equation>. The <heat equation> gives Gaussian <image smoothing>, while gradient-dependent or tensor-dependent diffusion can reduce transport across <image edges>. A <image smoothing> scale, stopping rule and appropriate boundary conditions are part of the filter.

= Diffusion tensor for image filtering
{parent=Diffusion image processing}
{title2=$u_t=\operatorname{div}(D\nabla u)$}

A positive tensor $D=a_ne_ne_n^T+a_te_te_t^T$ selects different diffusion strengths normal and tangent to an estimated <image edge>. Taking $0<a_n\ll a_t$ smooths along the <image edge> while reducing mixing across it. A <structure tensor> provides robust orientation estimates. Positive <eigenvalues> ensure forward local parabolicity.

= Image smoothing
{parent=Diffusion image processing}

<Image smoothing> suppresses rapid fluctuations attributed to noise. Linear <heat equation> <image smoothing> attenuates Fourier frequencies by $e^{-t|\xi|^2}$ but also blurs <image edges>. Nonlinear filtering uses <image signal> geometry to distinguish fluctuations from meaningful transitions.

= Median filter
{parent=Image smoothing}
{wiki}

A <median filter> replaces an <image signal> value by the <median> of nearby values. Unlike a linear average, it can reject isolated extreme observations without averaging both sides of an <image edge>. The small-radius response depends on neighbourhood geometry and measure; a uniform disk-area <median> and a circle-boundary <median> have different time scales.

= Disk-median curvature expansion
{parent=Median filter}
{title2=$\operatorname{median}_{B_h(x)}u=u+\tfrac16h^2|\nabla u|\operatorname{div}(\nabla u/|\nabla u|)+o(h^2)$}

At a smooth point with nonzero <gradient>, rotate coordinates so $\nabla u=G e_2$. Write $u=u_0+Gy+\tfrac12(Ax^2+2Bxy+Cy^2)+O(|(x,y)|^3)$. A candidate <median> $u_0+dh^2$ has level boundary $y=(dh^2-Ax^2/2)/G+O(h^3)$ in the disk. The area imbalance is $(2d-A/3)h^3/G+o(h^3)$; equal half-areas force $d=A/6$. Here $A=\Delta u-\nabla u^T(D^2u)\nabla u/|\nabla u|^2$ is the tangential second <derivative>, giving the formula. The disk uses uniform area measure, not its boundary measure.

= Gaussian blur
{c}
{parent=Image smoothing}
{wiki}
{title2=$G_\sigma*g$}

= Gaussian filtering
{c}
{synonym}

<Gaussian blur> applies <convolution> with $G_\sigma(x)=(2\pi\sigma^2)^{-n/2}e^{-|x|^2/(2\sigma^2)}$ to an <image signal>. It averages over a spatial scale $\sigma$, suppressing rapid variation while also blurring <image edges>. The <heat-kernel convolution> realizes this filter at time $t=\sigma^2/2$.

= Gaussian filtering Fourier multiplier
{c}
{parent=Gaussian blur}
{title2=$\widehat G_\sigma(\xi)=e^{-\sigma^2|\xi|^2/2}$}

The <Fourier transform of a Gaussian> and the <convolution theorem> give this multiplier with the angular-frequency convention $e^{-ix\cdot\xi}$. In cycles-per-length <frequencies> $k$, it is $e^{-2\pi^2\sigma^2|k|^2}$. Each coordinate has variance $\sigma^2$, and higher <frequencies> receive stronger attenuation.

= Variational image processing
{parent=Image processing}

A variational model balances agreement with observed data against a regularity or geometric penalty. Examples include <total variation denoising>, the <relaxed graph-area functional>, and the <Mumford–Shah functional>. Convex penalties support unique reconstruction, while segmentation energies can have multiple competing partitions.

= Mumford–Shah functional
{c}
{parent=Variational image processing}
{title2=$\int(u-g)^2+\alpha\int|\nabla u|^2+\beta\mathcal H^1(J_u)$}
{wiki}

= Mumford–Shah segmentation
{c}
{synonym}

= Mumford–Shah segmentation model
{c}
{synonym}

The energy $\int_{\Omega\setminus K}(u-g)^2+\alpha\int_{\Omega\setminus K}|\nabla u|^2+\beta\mathcal H^1(K)$ balances data fidelity, within-region smoothness and <image edge> length. In its relaxed <SBV space> formulation the <image edge> set is $J_u$. Clipping to the bounded data range, the <SBV compactness theorem> and <lower semicontinuity> prove existence; the full segmentation problem is not <strictly convex>.

= Ambrosio–Tortorelli approximation
{c}
{parent=Mumford–Shah functional}
{title2=$E_\varepsilon(u,v)$}

The <Mumford–Shah functional> can be approximated by an auxiliary edge field $0\le v\le1$ and energy
$$
E_\varepsilon=\int(u-g)^2+\alpha(v^2+\eta_\varepsilon)|\nabla u|^2+\beta\left[\varepsilon|\nabla v|^2+\frac{(1-v)^2}{4\varepsilon}\right],\qquad0<\eta_\varepsilon=o(\varepsilon).
$$
The field is near one inside regions and near zero across an <image edge>, weakening smoothing there. The profile $v(s)=1-e^{-|s|/(2\varepsilon)}$ has unit edge energy per unit length in this normalization. The https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1002/cpa.3160430805[1990 construction] is a <Gamma-convergence> approximation; its global-minimizer <limit> does not guarantee global optimality of an alternating numerical solve. Each fixed-field subproblem is quadratic, but the joint energy is nonconvex.

= Segmentation overfitting without an edge penalty
{parent=Mumford–Shah functional}

Removing the jump-length cost from the <Mumford–Shah functional> lets finer piecewise-constant partitions reduce squared fidelity without charging for their proliferating <image edges>. For <continuous> data on a compact image domain, fine-cell averages converge in squared error while the ordinary within-cell <gradient> is zero. A finite interface penalty makes that geometry costly.

= Nonconvexity of Mumford–Shah segmentation
{parent=Mumford–Shah functional}

On the unit square, let $g=0$ and take $u_0=\chi_{\{x_1>0.4\}}$, $u_1=\chi_{\{x_1>0.6\}}$. Both have one unit-length jump and zero ordinary <gradient>. Their mean has two unit-length jumps. For squared-error coefficient one, $[E(u_0)+E(u_1)]/2=0.5+\beta$ while $E((u_0+u_1)/2)=0.45+2\beta$, violating <convexity> when $\beta>0.05$. The <Mumford–Shah functional> can therefore be nonconvex even though the fixed-edge reconstruction subproblem is <strictly convex>.

= Fixed-edge Euler-Lagrange equation for Mumford–Shah
{parent=Mumford–Shah functional}
{title2=$u-\alpha\Delta u=g,\quad\partial_nu=0$}

For a fixed sufficiently regular <image edge> set, the <Mumford–Shah functional> is a <strictly convex> quadratic energy on the <Sobolev space> of its complement. A <first variation> gives $u-\alpha\Delta u=g$ within each region and separate one-sided <Neumann boundary conditions> on free edges. A jump need not vanish across an edge. The weak solution is unique by the <Lax-Milgram theorem>; this does not prove uniqueness when the edge set is also optimized.

= Edge-free Mumford–Shah limit
{parent=Mumford–Shah functional}
{title2=$u-\alpha\Delta u=g$}

As the jump-length weight tends to infinity with the <gradient> weight fixed, the limiting problem minimizes $\|u-g\|_2^2+\alpha\|\nabla u\|_2^2$ on $H^1$. It has a unique <minimizer> by the <Lax-Milgram theorem> and strict convexity. Without prescribed boundary values, its weak equation is $u-\alpha\Delta u=g$ with natural <Neumann boundary conditions>.

= Piecewise-constant Mumford–Shah problem
{parent=Mumford–Shah functional}
{title2=$\min_{\nabla u=0}\{\|u-g\|_2^2+\beta\mathcal H^1(J_u)\}$}

The infinite gradient-weight limit restricts to <SBV space> <image signals> with $\nabla u=0$ almost everywhere. Their energy is fidelity plus jump length, represented by constants on a <Caccioppoli partition>. Internal perimeter is counted once by $\tfrac12\sum_i\operatorname{Per}(E_i;\Omega)$; adjacent equal-valued regions can be merged.

= Piecewise-constant segmentation contrast threshold
{parent=Piecewise-constant Mumford–Shah problem}
{title2=$\beta L<\frac{a(A-a)}A d^2$}

Suppose two constant data intensities differ by $d$, have areas $a$ and $A-a$, and share an internal boundary of length $L$. Keeping the boundary costs $\beta L$ with zero fidelity; merging the two regions costs $a(A-a)d^2/A$ after fitting their common mean. The displayed inequality makes splitting cheaper among these two candidate geometries. This comparison does not prove either geometry is globally optimal among all partitions.

= Segmentation interface curvature balance
