Read 3 brings in <cache block> 2 and displaces <cache block> 0, leaving the other <cache slot> unused. Read 4 consequently misses at address 0, whereas the <fully associative cache> still has <cache block> 0. That read is a <conflict miss>: increasing placement freedom at the same capacity removes it. The complete <direct-mapped cache> trace has eight <cache misses>: four <compulsory misses>, three <capacity misses> and one <conflict miss>.

The convention here classifies each <cache miss> by first asking whether its <cache block> has ever been requested. If so, compare with an initially empty <fully associative cache> of the same capacity and <cache-line> size using <LRU replacement> on the same <memory access trace>: a miss there is a <capacity miss>, while a hit there identifies a <conflict miss> in the restricted design. This makes the comparison policy explicit; “capacity” is not a claim that no other replacement choice could save that individual read. The three categories describe this read-only demand model; external invalidations or fetching ahead of requests require additional reasoning. <Dive into Systems by Suzanne J. Matthews and others/11.4. CPU Caches>{ref}

= Cache miss rate
{parent=Cache miss}
{title2=$m=M/R$}

The fraction of $R>0$ <memory references> yielding <cache misses>, with $M$ their count: $m=M/R$. When each reference is classified as exactly one <cache hit> or <cache miss>, $h+m=1$; the two-slot <fully associative cache> example has $m=7/12$.

= Capacity miss
{parent=Cache miss}

A repeated <cache-block> access that misses even in the comparison <fully associative cache> of the same capacity and <cache-line> size using <LRU replacement>. In the <memory cache> example these are reads 6, 7 and 11. The replacement assumption matters to this classification.

= Conflict miss
{parent=Cache miss}

= Placement miss
{synonym}

A repeated <cache-block> access that misses in a restricted design but hits in the comparison <fully associative cache> of the same capacity and <cache-line> size using <LRU replacement>. Read 4 of the <direct-mapped cache> comparison under <cache miss> isolates this effect while another <cache slot> is unused.

= Cache miss penalty
{parent=Cache miss}
{title2=$p$}

The additional <memory access latency> charged for a <cache miss> beyond a common lookup time in the <average memory access time> model. It includes obtaining the requested <cache block> and making the read complete; this model excludes extra costs from writes and overlapping requests.

= Compulsory miss
{parent=Cache miss}

A <cache miss> on the first access to a <cache block> in an initially empty <memory cache> with no fetching ahead of requests. Larger capacity cannot eliminate these first fetches; subsequent <cache misses> can have other causes.

= Fully associative cache
{parent=Cache (computing)}

A <memory cache> allowing any <cache block> to occupy any resident slot. When full, its replacement policy chooses a victim from all resident <cache lines>. The examples in <temporal locality> and <spatial locality> use <LRU replacement>, so no additional placement restriction causes eviction.

The diagram enumerates every read of the fixed <memory access trace> under <memory cache>. Keep <cache slots> fixed: fill the lowest-numbered unused slot first, and subsequently reuse the victim's slot. Separately list recency from least recently used to most recently used. A <cache hit> refreshes that recency without moving the resident data to another <cache slot>.

\Image[fully-associative-lru-read-trace.png]
{title=Every read, <cache hit> or <cache miss>, eviction, <cache slot> and recency state of the synthetic two-slot <fully associative cache> with <LRU replacement>}

After read 3, <cache block> 0 is older than <cache block> 2. Read 4 hits <cache block> 0, making <cache block> 2 older; read 5 must therefore evict <cache block> 2 to load <cache block> 1. Omitting recency updates on <cache hits> would choose the wrong victim. Read 6 immediately needs the evicted <cache block> 2, showing that <LRU replacement> uses past demand rather than knowledge of future demand.

With capacity four <cache slots> and the same <cache-line> size, the same <memory access trace> has just its four <compulsory misses> and eight <cache hits>, since all four distinct <cache blocks> fit. Greater capacity helps this trace, but full associativity alone does not make <LRU replacement> optimal. Its attraction is freedom from placement restrictions; a hardware implementation must still identify a matching <cache tag> among eligible entries, and exact recency tracking adds work. <Memory part 2: CPU caches by Ulrich Drepper>{ref}

= Direct-mapped cache
{parent=Cache (computing)}

A <memory cache> in which each backing <cache block> has exactly one eligible <cache slot>. The example under <cache miss> maps <cache block> $q$ to slot $q\bmod2$: <cache blocks> 0 and 2 compete for slot 0 despite free space in slot 1.

= Memory access latency
{parent=Cache (computing)}
{title2=$t$}

The time from a <memory reference>'s request until the requested data become available. <Average memory access time> averages this delay in the stated serial model; it does not directly measure whole-program runtime.

= Average memory access time
{parent=Memory access latency}
{title2=$t_h+mp$}

= AMAT
{c}
{synonym}

The <arithmetic mean> of <memory access latencies>. Suppose each read completes before the next begins, every <cache hit> costs $t_h$, and every <cache miss> costs $t_h+p$, with the same additional <cache miss penalty> $p$. If $h$ is <cache hit rate> and $m=1-h$ is <cache miss rate>, the <weighted arithmetic mean> is
$$
\operatorname{AMAT}=h\,t_h+m(t_h+p)=t_h+mp.
$$
The common lookup cost is paid on both outcomes. This additive assumption matches the simple model in <Operating Systems: Three Easy Pieces by Remzi H. Arpaci-Dusseau and others/Beyond Physical Memory: Policies> (§22.1, equation 22.1, pp. 1–2), where main <computer memory> acts as a <memory cache> for <memory pages> backed by disk; here the same accounting is applied to a toy data <memory cache>.

Assign synthetic values $t_h=2\,\mathrm{ns}$ and $p=30\,\mathrm{ns}$. The twelve-read, two-slot <fully associative cache> example has five <cache hits> and seven <cache misses>, giving
$$
\operatorname{AMAT}=2+\frac{7}{12}\,30=19.5\,\mathrm{ns},\qquad T=5\cdot2+7\cdot32=234\,\mathrm{ns}.
$$
With four <cache slots>, its four <compulsory misses> instead give $\operatorname{AMAT}=12\,\mathrm{ns}$. This comparison holds the timing costs fixed as capacity changes, which is a model assumption rather than a hardware promise.

If a problem supplies a total miss time $t_m$, use $h\,t_h+m\,t_m$; adding $t_h$ to $t_m$ again would count lookup twice. Variable <cache miss penalties>, multiple consulted levels, or overlapping requests require a richer timing model. Real <CPU> work can overlap a pending read, so this <arithmetic mean> cannot by itself predict program speed or stall time. <Memory part 2: CPU caches by Ulrich Drepper>{ref}
